- In economics, we define the short run for a firm as the time period over which some factors of production are fixed. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71nsacdrajyjdm72ewnmxhj))
    - Note: abbreviated SR, in my notes. Here most of the fixed costs are considered untouchable
- Typically, we assume that capital is fixed in the short run so that a firm cannot change its scale of operations (plant and equipment) over the short run ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71nt9y0y25jpq36m6d2xsy6))
    - Note: so no loans, and cannot make any serious altering decisions
- All factors of production (costs) are variable in the long run ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71ntzn0vn9phv2wx2ah4fx4))
    - Note: Everyone is on the chopping block, abbrevaited LR (long run)
- The firm can let its leases expire and sell its equipment, thereby avoiding costs that are fixed in the short run. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71nw188a09xm6f6a7dxbs35))
- Shutdown and Breakeven Under Perfect Competition ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71nw4yjkxdppabereyp6m3a))
- As a simple example of shutdown and breakeven analysis, consider a retail store with a one-year lease (fixed cost) and one employee (quasi-fixed cost), so that variable costs are simply the store’s cost of merchandise. If the total sales (total revenue) just cover both fixed and variable costs, price equals both average revenue and average total cost —so we are at the breakeven output quantity, and economic profit equals zero. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71ny1nx73xw7hctpd5gjs1p))
    - Note: example
- During the period of the lease (the short run), as long as items are being sold for more than their variable cost, the store should continue to operate to minimize losses. If items are being sold for less than their average variable cost, losses would be reduced by shutting down the business in the short run.
  In the long run, a firm should shut down if the price is less than average total cost, regardless of the relation between price and average variable cost. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71nymak1yw1nnexha7dq8kp))
    - Note: this is important to understand in the context of examples. The real thing to remember will be below where we compave AVC and ATC. But this is just an example to use to remind me of the context of where we are working
- For a firm under perfect competition (a price-taker), we can use a graph of cost functions to examine the profitability of the firm at different output prices ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71p7hfd9rz33pkcft4f5wpp))
    - Note: firsm is in near perfect competition with a lot of competitiors, which makeit impossible to set price, rather have to accept whatever price is set in the market

    - Note: gap between p1 and p2 is the zone in which you operate in SR and shutdown in LR
- Because some costs are fixed in the short run, it will be better for the firm to continue production in the short run as long as average revenue is greater than average variable costs. At prices between P1 and P2 in Figure 12.1, the firm has losses, but the losses are smaller than would occur if all production were stopped. A ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71qzgegwcacme59am5kqdzh))
    - Note: losses would be less in SR as you have already paid for the fixed costs, better to keep covering your variable cost and keep trying to move up.
- As long as total revenue is greater than total variable cost, at least some of the firm’s fixed costs are covered by continuing to produce and sell its product. If the firm were to shut down, losses would be equal to the fixed costs that still must be paid. As long as price is greater than average variable costs, the firm will minimize its losses in the short run by continuing in business. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71r14ccj2151vy1g2dkcrg9))
- short-run shutdown point ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71r1vyj9k3a3rkvx1y6zbw0))
    - Note: point at which you shut down in the short run
- long-run shutdown point ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71r27jb9z389b4hwwk7nwbj))
    - Note: point at which you shutdown iin the long run
- If average revenue is just equal to average total cost, total revenue is just equal to total (economic) cost, and this is the firm’s breakeven point. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71r2nczw8jxgr5q8dzqfgtq))
    - Note: average revenue is equal to average total cost, AR goes up you making moneyyy
- To sum up, if average revenue is less than average variable cost in the short run, the firm should shut down. This is its short-run shutdown point. If average revenue is greater than average variable cost in the short run, the firm should continue to operate, even if it has losses. In the long run, the firm should shut down if average revenue is less than average total cost. This is the long-run shutdown point. If average revenue is just equal to average total cost, total revenue is just equal to total (economic) cost, and this is the firm’s breakeven point.
  If AR ≥ ATC, the firm should stay in the market in both the short and long run.
  If AR ≥ AVC, but AR < ATC, the firm should stay in the market in the short run but will exit the market in the long run.
  If AR < AVC, the firm should shut down in the short run and exit the market in the long run. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71r1kagg62q1vd7nnsfty15))
- If AR ≥ ATC, the firm should stay in the market in both the short and long run. If AR ≥ AVC, but AR < ATC, the firm should stay in the market in the short run but will exit the market in the long run.
  If AR < AVC, the firm should shut down in the short run and exit the market in the long run. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71r3f1j0mppvjs9h7275ppc))
    - Note: AR = Average Revenue
      ATC = Average Total Cost 
      AVC = Average Variable Cost

- We can, however, still identify the conditions under which a firm is breaking even, should shut down in the short run, and should shut down in the long run in terms of total costs and total revenue. These conditions are as follows: TR = TC: break even TC > TR > TVC: firm should continue to operate in the short run but shut down in the long run TR < TVC: firm should shut down in the short run and the long run ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71r70zt4s2gcd03gabf666r))
    - Note: TR = Total Revenue 
      TC = Total Cost 
      TVC = Total Variable Cost

- The lowest point on the LRATC corresponds to the scale or plant size at which the average total cost of production is at a minimum. This scale is sometimes called the minimum efficient scale ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71rvbnax1j3qh0zmbtqkswy))

      ofcourse there is no end to this optimisation but for that product being manuactured using those variables, this is as cheap as it gets
- Under perfect competition, firms must operate at minimum efficient scale in long-run equilibrium, and LRATC will equal the market price. Recall that under perfect competition, firms earn zero economic profit in long-run equilibrium. Firms that have chosen a different scale of operations with higher average total costs will have economic losses and must either leave the industry or change to the minimum efficient scale. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71rxzaznbn6kf57jgmz9fe7))
    - Note: this is again assuming you are competing on the very same product with the very same specifications, this is almost never the case. Which is why you use marketing, etc to differentiate the products

- The price/output decision for monopolistic competition is illustrated in Figure 12.5. Panel A of Figure 12.5 illustrates the short-run price/output characteristics of monopolistic competition for a single firm. As indicated, firms in monopolistic competition maximize economic profits by producing where marginal revenue (MR) equals marginal cost (MC), and by charging the price for that quantity from the demand curve, D. Here, the firm earns positive economic profits because price, P*, exceeds average total cost, ATC*. Due to low barriers to entry, competitors can enter the market in pursuit of these economic profits. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71wadnfvd9g8v8gq55pmjt1))
- ![](https://readwise-assets.s3.amazonaws.com/media/reader/pub/5aab978a0f212cf3dfc81cd25f7b9c95.png) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71wbaaf8seeb72jyvnnvmh1))
    - Note: in the short run you find the price at which you intercept with the average total cost, then you push that up or down to the demand curve and you charge that price here in my opinion, the company could have charged lower in the short run 
      in the long run we see that ATC = price, again we make a profit as price is hgiher than MR=MC point but lower than in the SR
- Panel B of Figure 12.5 illustrates long-run equilibrium for a representative firm after new firms have entered the market. As indicated, the entry of new firms shifts the demand curve faced by each individual firm down to the point where price equals average total cost (P* = ATC*), such that economic profit is zero. At this point, there is no longer an incentive for new firms to enter the market, and long-run market equilibrium is established. A firm in monopolistic competition continues to produce at the quantity where MR = MC, but it no longer earns positive economic profits. We can get to a similar long-run equilibrium even without entry of new firms if each firm increases its marketing spending (a component of ATC) to either increase or defend its market share until ATC has increased to that shown in Panel B. If all firms compete in this way, each firm will produce Q* and sell at P* but earn no economic profit because marketing costs have increased ATC to ATC*, which is equal to price. Advertising expenses are often relatively high for firms in monopolistic competition. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71whsf08sm8yp9chyfnzsn0))
- Note that with monopolistic competition, price is greater than MC (i.e., producers can realize an economic profit), average total cost is not at a minimum for the quantity produced (suggesting excess capacity, or an inefficient scale of production), and the price is slightly higher than under perfect competition. - One traditional model of oligopoly, the kinked demand curve model, is based on the assumption that competitors are unlikely to match a price increase by a competitor, but very likely to match a price decrease by a competitor ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71wtet6dp2vvgg2qvxa95wh))
- This results in a kink in the demand curves faced by each producer, at the current market price. Each firm believes that it faces a demand curve that is more elastic (flatter) above the current price (the kink in the demand curve) than it is below the given price. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71wtrdrzwnrdm0dwb1m0shj))
- . The kink price is at price PK, where a firm produces QK. A firm believes that if it raises its price above PK, its competitors will remain at PK, and it will lose market share because it has the highest price. Above PK, the demand curve is considered to be relatively elastic (i.e., a small price increase will result in a large decrease in demand). On the other hand, if a firm decreases its price below PK, other firms will match the price cut, and all firms will experience a relatively small increase in sales relative to any price reduction. Therefore, QK is the profit-maximizing level of output. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71wvs4b0shqn9awqbv6nxz4))

- We say that the decisions of firms in an oligopoly are interdependent; that is, the pricing decision of one firm depends on the pricing decisions of other firms. Some models of market price equilibrium have a set of rules for the actions of oligopolists.
  These rules assume they choose prices based on the choices of the other firms. By specifying the decision rules that each firm follows, we can design a model that allows us to determine the equilibrium prices and quantities for firms operating in an oligopoly market. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x3ac3ktvw04338pf095tq))
- An early model of oligopoly pricing decisions is the Cournot model. In Cournot’s duopoly model, two firms with identical MC curves each choose their preferred selling price based on the price the other firm chose in the previous period. Firms assume that the competitor’s price will not change. The long-run equilibrium for an oligopoly with two firms (duopoly), in the Cournot model, is for both firms to sell the same quantity, dividing the market equally at the equilibrium price. The equilibrium price is less than the price that a single monopolist would charge, but greater than the equilibrium price that would result under perfect competition. With a greater number of producers, the long-run market equilibrium price moves toward the competitive price. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x4azmp2b339k8rphafh02))
- Another model, the Stackelberg model, uses a different set of rules and produces a different result. While the Cournot model assumes the competitors choose price simultaneously each period, the Stackelberg model assumes pricing decisions are made sequentially. One firm, the “leader,” chooses its price first, and the other firm chooses a price based on the leader’s price. In long-run equilibrium, under these rules, the leader charges a higher price and receives a greater proportion of the firms’ total profits. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x4vhxzr6raqx83wvdrk8e))
- These models are early versions of rules-based models, which fall under the heading of what are now generally termed strategic games. Strategic games comprise decision ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x53vt11pdyzmta59m7bnr))
- models in which the best choice for a firm depends on the expected actions (reactions) of other firms. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x5664t91pcn6fvct0cdyc))
- A more general model of strategic games was developed by Nobel Prize winner John Nash, who developed the concept of a Nash equilibrium. A Nash equilibrium is reached when the choices of all firms are such that there is no other choice that makes any firm better off (increases profits or decreases losses). The Cournot model results in a Nash equilibrium. In equilibrium, neither competitor can increase profits by changing the price they charge. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x5n0tkdj52n2gff13p16x))
- Figure 12.9, which shows the choices and resulting profits for two firms. Each firm can charge either a high price or a low price. If both firms charge a high price, Firm A earns 1,000 and Firm B earns 600. While Firm A would not charge the low price (it would earn less regardless of Firm B’s decision), Firm B can increase profits to 700 by charging a low price. With Firm A charging a high price and Firm B charging a low price, neither firm can increase profits by unilaterally changing its price strategy. Thus, we can identify the Nash equilibrium in this scenario as Firm B charging a low price and Firm A charging a high price. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x6e30n3cnwz2354mfttgh))
- We can illustrate the potential of cooperative behavior among producers to increase the total profits of all firms in the market. Collusion refers to competitors making a joint agreement to charge a given price—or, alternatively, to agree to specific levels of output. In Figure 12.9, the greatest joint profits (1,600) are earned when both firms charge a high price. If Firm A offers to pay Firm B 200 for charging a high price, Firm A’s profits increase from 600 to 1,000. After paying 200 to Firm B, Firm A still gains 200. Firm B’s profits (including the payment of 200) increase from 700 to 800. Collusion, in this case, increases the profits of both firms, compared to the Nash equilibrium. If firms can enter into and enforce an agreement regarding pricing and output, often they can all benefit. Such agreements among producers are illegal in many countries because they reduce competition.
  An example of a collusive agreement is the OPEC cartel. Cartel-member countries agree to restrict their oil production to increase the world price of oil. Members sometimes choose to “cheat” on the cartel agreement by producing more than the amount of oil they have agreed to produce. If members of a cartel do not adhere to the agreement (taking advantage of the higher market price but failing to restrict output to the agreed-upon amount), the agreement can quickly break down. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x8kyae2j6htn2xtcte4rj))
- In general, collusive agreements to increase price in an oligopoly market will be more successful (have less cheating) under the following conditions: ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x8xaqz0n18ph7h2rz5zzy))
- There are fewer firms. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x8yyxdny893j1nhrg56mc))
- Products are more similar (less differentiated). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x90fx4rzjxyzceetwzxnf))
- Cost structures are more similar. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x91xr51b09qjx8dg14950))
- Purchases are relatively small and frequent. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x94p14tget41gy9tjcka9))
- Retaliation by other firms for cheating is more certain and more severe. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x9gw4my3ybnf90k3750pj))
- There is less actual or potential competition from firms outside the cartel. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71x9mdzv1xxsktdwqwa71gh))
- A final model of oligopoly behavior to consider is the dominant firm model. In this model, a single firm has a significantly large market share because of its greater scale and lower cost structure—the dominant firm (DF). In such a model, the market price is essentially determined by the DF, and the other competitive firms (CFs) take this market price as given. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71xa1zvq74a83cweqtw4505))
- The DF believes that the quantity supplied by the other firms decreases at lower prices, so that the DF’s demand curve is related to the market demand curve, as shown in Figure 12.10. Based on this demand curve (DDF) and its associated marginal revenue (MRDF) curve, the firm will maximize profits at a price of P*. The CFs maximize profits by producing the quantity for which their marginal cost (MCCF) equals P*, quantity QCF. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71xaf4rh9bvn2nsmxzw3wqm))
- A price decrease by one of the CFs, which increases QCF in the short run, will lead to a decrease in price by the DF, and CFs will decrease output or exit the industry in the long run. The long-run result of such a price decrease by competitors below P* would then be to decrease the overall market share of competitor firms and increase the market share of the DF. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71xnsc0ercg5x3z45mef31p))
- Clearly, oligopoly markets exhibit many possible outcomes that depend on the characteristics of the firms and of the market itself. The important point is that the firms’ decisions are interdependent so that the expected reactions of other firms are an important consideration. Overall, the resulting price will be somewhere between the price based on perfect collusion that would maximize total profits to all firms in the market (which is actually the monopoly price), and the price that would result from perfect competition and generate zero economic profits in the long run. These two limiting outcomes are illustrated in Figure 12.11 as Pcollusion with Qcollusion for perfect collusion, and Pcompetition and Qcompetition for perfect competition ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k71xpbhthvgqwfn5qzegnt7g))

- Consequently, regulators often use market shares (percentages of market sales) to measure the degree of monopoly or market power of firms in an industry ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72c15p2f33bdrctywmfx3mr))
- Often, mergers or acquisitions of companies in the same industry or market are not permitted by government authorities when they determine that the market share of the combined firms will be too high and, therefore, detrimental to the economy. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72c1bn8kf0ggt8a7b5jje5a))
- Market or industry concentration measures are often used as an indicator of market power. One concentration measure is the N-firm concentration ratio, which is calculated as the sum of the percentage market shares of the largest N firms in a market ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72c1ngz85cwyddh2cxhmvtc))
- While this measure is simple to calculate and understand, it does not directly measure market power or elasticity of demand. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72c1rywkr3wskza6dpjftc2))
- One limitation of the N-firm concentration ratio is that it may be relatively insensitive to mergers of firms within an industry. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72c1xp7s74km6q9mx4k3acd))
- This problem is reduced by using an alternative measure of market concentration, the Herfindahl-Hirschman Index (HHI) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72c22saexvj63ag4kd02rsd))
- The HHI is calculated as the sum of the squares of the market shares of the largest firms in the marke ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72c27t22ef4x5bz83pq54jk))
- EXAMPLE: 4-firm concentration ratio and 4-firm HHI Given the market shares of the following firms, calculate the 4-firm concentration ratio and the 4-firm HHI, both before and after a merger of Acme and Blake. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72c2tzdect345b84mefb9vx))
- ![](https://readwise-assets.s3.amazonaws.com/media/reader/pub/6aff3e0c9a5232a512fc57938528d68a.png) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72c3h0taynh9313gy88v5zc))
- Answer: Before the merger, the 4-firm concentration ratio for the market is 25 + 15 + 15 + 10 = 65%. After the merger, the Acme + Blake firm has 40% of the market, and the 4- firm concentration ratio is 40 + 15 + 10 + 5 = 70%. Although the 4-firm concentration ratio has only increased slightly, the market power of the largest firm in the industry has increased significantly, from 25% to 40%.
  Before the merger, the 4-firm HHI is 0.252 + 0.152 + 0.152 + 0.102 = 0.1175.
  After the merger, the 4-firm HHI is 0.402 + 0.152 + 0.102 + 0.052 = 0.1950, a significant increase. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72c318rycd8tb7446fzyhh6))
- A limitation that applies to both of our simple concentration measures is that barriers to entry are not considered in either case. Even a firm with high market share may not have much pricing power if barriers to entry are low and there is potential competition. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72c3bptj8b0ycf5kfg2w9yv))
    - Note: the closer you move to perfect competition, the less pricing power you have
- With low barriers to entry, it may be the case that other firms stand ready to enter the market if firms currently in the market attempt to increase prices significantly ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72c4b851a1ktcvhcz9mqk24))
- In this case, the elasticity of demand for existing firms may be high, even though they have relatively high market shares and industry concentration measures. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72c4e6p30px70jte6rrwrpm))

- READING 13 UNDERSTANDING BUSINESS CYCLES ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72cg4te7778k9k7bzzty6dx))
- MODULE 13.1: BUSINESS CYCLES ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72cg7d38za2jrx3qcyqc06a))
- The business cycle is characterized by fluctuations in economic activity ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72cgcb5xnj9twk584ycnmwg))
- The business cycle has four phases: expansion (real GDP is increasing), ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72cmqh01gzd3hhc5mpezw0z))
- peak (real GDP stops increasing and begins decreasing) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72cmxp063acsctjcsz0pe1f))
- contraction or recession (real GDP is decreasing) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72cn13fx74j7m8cvs5yrqys))
- trough (real GDP stops decreasing and begins increasing) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72cn4znqrvhvn0cpne44dqa))
- ![](https://readwise-assets.s3.amazonaws.com/media/reader/pub/fec4f062dc3ab4ce7924210e284fa7d6.png) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72cp751cjtbknj6xczbc1fg))
- The curve in the figure illustrates the classical cycle, which is based on real GDP relative to a beginning value ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72crmy84se8kprkjf3f480m))
- The growth cycle refers to changes in the percentage difference between real GDP and its longer-term trend or potential value (shown as the “average” line in the figure) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72cxgheqhy9dqtns5m64sdp))
    - Note: the real gdp is the blue line and the black line is the long term trend or potential
- The growth rate cycle refers to changes in the annualized percentage growth rate from one period to the next and tends to show both peaks and troughs earlier than the other two measures ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72cxpz38d90p7fp179q6s7z))
    - Note: difference between yoy or or annualised percentage growth, from one period to the next
- The growth rate cycle—which, like the growth cycle, shows GDP relative to a trend rate—is the preferred measure for economists and practitioners. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72dv8fsjza02gtex7xahj0n))
- An expansion features growth in most sectors of the economy, with increasing employment, consumer spending, and business investment. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72dvw1qjx1mp72nbd180y3y))
- As an expansion approaches ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72dvyk110q4nvpj2mk504ag))
- its peak, the rates of increase in spending, investment, and employment slow but remain positive, while inflation accelerates. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72dxt531jn2httshn9brrhn))
- A contraction or recession is associated with declines in most sectors, with inflation typically decreasing ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72dxwhhv6tebkdx1pzfny80))
- When a contraction reaches a trough and the economy begins a new expansion or recovery, economic growth becomes positive again and inflation is typically moderate, but employment growth might not start to increase until the expansion has taken hold convincingly. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k72dy2akxqv99ch621n6rg4d))
- Economists commonly consider two consecutive quarters of growth in real GDP to be the beginning of an expansion, and two consecutive quarters of declining real GDP to be the beginning of a contraction. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73rtyzfe6f3k37fv65gx50y))
- Statistical agencies that date expansions and recessions, such as the National Bureau of Economic Research in the United States, look at a wider variety of additional economic data—especially unemployment, industrial production, and inflation—to identify turning points in the business cycle. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73rv57pkzav8jm71nn3mysp))
- A key aspect of business cycles is that they recur, but not at regular intervals. Past business cycles have been as short as a year, or longer than a decade. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73rvf05k1xek7mf3qrw6wsx))
- The idea of a business cycle applies to economies that consist mainly of businesses. For economies that are mostly subsistence agriculture or dominated by state planning, fluctuations in activity are not really “business cycles” in the sense we are discussing here ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73rvsxkc22ax4fj13rgesv6))
    - Note: for capitalistic economies running on any kind of business, business cycles are important, but for less market orientated economies business cycles arent are straightforward as this
- Credit cycles refer to cyclical fluctuations in interest rates and the availability of loans (credit). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73rxfyp1wv35dyp0qja1twg))
- Typically, lenders are more willing to lend, and tend to offer lower interest rates, during economic expansions. Conversely, they are less willing to lend, and require higher interest rates, when the economy is slowing (contracting). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73rxma9tk8feb1jhes576dx))
- Credit cycles may amplify business cycles. Widely available or “loose” credit conditions during expansions can lead to “bubbles” (prices based on implausible expectations) in the markets for some assets, such as subprime mortgages in the period leading up to the financial crisis of 2007–2009 ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73sjxmm287ce7bxypwqxex3))
- Some research suggests that expansions tend to be stronger, and contractions deeper and longer lasting, when they coincide with credit cycles. They do not always coincide, however, as historical data suggest credit cycles have been longer in duration than business cycles on average ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73sk98sq2a6qw561fzdkyg8))
- Business Cycles and Resource Use Fluctuation ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73skf85wq685jk83vh0d0r1))
- Inventories are an important business cycle indicator ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73skpcg1g84e9t9gzxc2j94))
- Firms try to keep enough inventory on hand to meet sales demand but do not want to keep too much of their capital tied up in inventory. As a result, the inventory-sales ratio in many industries tends toward a normal level in times of steady economic growth. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73skwrvngjvchhkcc990gh9))
- When an expansion is approaching its peak, sales growth begins to slow, and unsold inventories accumulate. This can be seen in an increase in the inventory-sales ratio above its normal level. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73sm897qvhc6tvs175m8zw9))
- Firms respond to this unplanned increase in inventory by reducing production, which is one of the causes of the subsequent contraction in the economy ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73smgzsz81thab9fk8hp2y2))
- . An increase in inventories is counted in the GDP statistics as economic output whether the increase is planned or unplanned. An analyst who looks only at GDP growth, rather than the inventory-sales ratio, might see economic strength rather than the beginning of weakness. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t30p4263ecdr3vecr5kx8))
- The opposite occurs when a contraction reaches its trough. Having reduced their production levels to adjust for lower sales demand, firms find their inventories becoming depleted more quickly once sales growth begins to accelerate. This causes the inventory-sales ratio to decrease below its normal level. To meet the increase in demand, firms will increase output, and the inventory-sales ratio will increase back toward normal levels. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t39zrvcp0949hpc0nsptx))
- One of the ways that firms react to fluctuations in business activity is by adjusting their use of labor and physical capital. Adding and subtracting workers in lockstep with changes in economic growth would be costly for firms, in terms of both direct expenses and the damage it would do to employee morale and loyalty. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t3j6d1030x3a88xh5kerw))
- Instead, firms typically begin by changing how they use their current workers, producing less or more output per hour or adjusting the hours they work by adding or removing overtime. Only when an expansion or contraction appears likely to persist will they hire or lay off workers ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t3t2z86zr5z1grjyrw8v2))
- Similarly, because it is costly to adjust production levels by frequently buying and selling plant and equipment, firms first adjust their production levels by using their existing physical capital more or less intensively. As an expansion persists, firms will increase their production capacity by investing more in plant and equipment. During contractions, however, firms will not necessarily sell plant and equipment outright.
  They can reduce their physical capacity by spending less on maintenance or by delaying the replacement of equipment that is nearing the end of its useful life. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t4d1x1awanrbhp38ssr7g))
- Consumer Sector Activity ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t4hmtwry4cq537sz9hkwd))
- Consumer spending, the largest component of gross domestic product, depends on the level of consumers’ current incomes and their expectations about their future incomes. As a result, consumer spending increases during expansions and decreases during contractions. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t4x9s04cgcsscjq6ksdkn))
- Consumer spending in some sectors is more sensitive to business cycle phases than spending in other sectors ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t57mewvshhabqfr49awje))
- Spending on durable goods is highly cyclical because they are often higher-value purchases ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t5cc0prbcqzacsjvqqrqa))
- Consumers are more willing to purchase high-value durable goods (e.g., appliances, furniture, automobiles) during expansions, when incomes are increasing and economic confidence is high ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t5jh8rxt19pkmfmc8zqdr))
- During contractions (and sometimes extending into the early stages of expansions), consumers often postpone durable goods purchases until they are more confident about their employment status and prospects for income growth. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t5pfr9r2czrc2e1xsj695))
- Consumer spending on services is also positively correlated with business cycle phases, but not to the same extent as durable goods spending. Services include some ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t61v2drsnkm8stnc348za))
- discretionary spending, such as for travel, lodging, and restaurant meals, but they also include spending that is less discretionary, such as for telecommunications, health care, and insurance. Spending on nondurable goods, such as food at home or household products for everyday use, remains relatively stable over the business cycle. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t6a91q8vx5vnh1w0fr7az))
- Housing Sector Activity ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t6e75rq7gs4685n7r940z))
- Although the housing sector is a smaller part of the economy relative to overall consumer spending, cyclical swings in activity in the housing market can be large, so that the effect on overall economic activity is greater than it otherwise would be. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t6ph6kbz0mr6vb9scvc3b))
- Important determinants of the level of economic activity in the housing sector are as follows: ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t6smyjvs6gbxey72e9kdz))
- 1. Mortgage rates. Low interest rates tend to increase home buying and construction, while high interest rates tend to reduce home buying and construction. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t723dmkk3z49jasc5zbbs))
- 2. Housing costs relative to income. When incomes are cyclically high (low) relative to home costs, including mortgage financing costs, home buying and construction tend to increase (decrease). Housing activity can decrease even when incomes are rising if home prices are rising faster than incomes, as often occurs late in expansions. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k73t7wceh5h0s0fgaasw2rzk))
- 3. Speculative activity. As we saw in the housing sector in 2007–08 in many economies, rising home prices can lead to purchases based on expectations of further gains. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74s92ywkze3m0tapk1crh5s))
    - Note: buying a house as if it is as safe and secure commodity, that has practically no risk associated wiht it
- Higher prices led to more construction and eventually excess building. This resulted in falling prices that decreased or eliminated speculative demand and led to dramatic decreases in house prices and in housing activity overall ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74sh9gfh3mzysk72ymsjjb9))
    - Note: as people were unable to continue paying for the lavish houses they had bought, or since prices werent going up as much as they should have, a lot of people had to drastically reduce the price they would be willing to sell at
- 4. Demographic factors. The proportion of the population in the 25- to 40-year-old segment is positively related to activity in the housing sector because these are the ages of greatest household formation. Strong population shifts from rural areas to cities, as may occur in newly industrializing economies, may require large increases in construction of new housing to accommodate those needs. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74sk08rcz78xaja6r3pxc86))
- External Trade Sector Activity ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74smhfnn9gvnt56y15hp6dt))
- The most important factors determining the level of a country’s imports and exports are domestic GDP growth, GDP growth of trading partners, and currency exchange rates. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74sqy83wphr4tete79qtt9j))
    - Note: GDP growth of partners really matters as their growth directly impacts your exports, and the imports you get from their currency flow
- Increasing growth of domestic GDP leads to increases in purchases of foreign goods (imports), while decreasing domestic GDP growth reduces imports. Exports depend on the growth rates of GDP of other economies (especially those of important trading partners). Increasing foreign incomes increase sales to foreigners (exports), and decreasing economic growth in foreign countries decreases domestic exports. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74ssd2nd2p112338t6nrjtp))
    - Note: increasing foreign incomes as in increase of income for foreign nationals in foreign countries
- An increase in the value of a country’s currency makes its goods more expensive to foreign buyers and foreign goods less expensive to domestic buyers, which tends to decrease exports and increase imports ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74t654ebg61ak3akykrxraa))
- A decrease in the value of a country’s currency has the opposite effect (increasing exports and decreasing imports) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74t71g4pnxeyhbs62e131k5))
- . Currencies affect import and export volumes over time in response to persistent trends in foreign exchange rates, rather than in response to short-term changes, which can be quite volatile. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74t75adq07an7b2px16w9sq))
    - Note: what this sentence means to say is that this is a long term trend, not something thagt changes very often. As countries currencies are very volatile and change very often, it is through decisions taken over a very long time do we find currency values, increasingly significantly enough that make these dynamics shifts possible
- Currency effects on imports and exports can differ in direction from GDP growth effects and respond to a complex set of variables. The effects of changes in GDP levels and growth rates are more direct and immediate. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74t9zx32gczr65jkw7yk61c))
- Trough: – The GDP growth rate changes from negative to positive.
  – There is a high unemployment rate, and an increasing use of overtime and temporary workers.
  – Spending on consumer durable goods and housing may increase.
  – There is a moderate or decreasing inflation rate. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74tcxzrk4rakp6g842zjm8p))
- Expansion: – The GDP growth rate increases.
  – The unemployment rate decreases as hiring accelerates.
  – There are investment increases in producers’ equipment and home construction.
  – The inflation rate may increase.
  – Imports increase as domestic income growth accelerates. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74td03h2nb35rprs2m38x1e))
- Peak: – The GDP growth rate decreases.
  – The unemployment rate decreases, but hiring slows.
  – Consumer spending and business investments grow at slower rates.
  – The inflation rate increases. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74td1vftxrynyp2n8wmhce2))
- Contraction/recession: – The GDP growth rate is negative.
  – Hours worked decrease; unemployment rate increases.
  – Consumer spending, home construction, and business investments decrease.
  – The inflation rate decreases with a lag.
  – Imports decrease as domestic income growth slows. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74td43m8rt4tr0e42wvrs4h))
- Economic Indicators ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74td5gpb369nfty7gdmx383))
- Economic indicators can be classified into three categories: leading indicators that have been known to change direction before peaks or troughs in the business cycle ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74tdpawz6vcnre4h9zgetvg))
- coincident indicators that change direction at roughly the same time as peaks or troughs ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74tdsqg27qwazdzskae8jpj))
- lagging indicators that tend not to change direction until after expansions or contractions are already underway. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74tdwt7mz10s8xf8d61dwwv))

- READING 14 FISCAL POLICY ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74tz5pk3maxm8pkf5xydn77))
- MODULE 14.1: FISCAL POLICY OBJECTIVES ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74tz8kp59kxmbf0arwyx2tw))
- Fiscal policy refers to a government’s use of spending and taxation to influence economic activity. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74tzchsvk4g8hsyjxcg12dh))
- The budget is said to be balanced when tax revenues equal government expenditures ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74tzfm7vv5mhvqq3btyfwn7))
- A budget surplus occurs when government tax revenues exceed expenditures ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74tzk7zkhamrh7yy6z76am3))
- a budget deficit occurs when government expenditures exceed tax revenues ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74tzpbth0t4t6ttxrx03cf0))
- An increase in the deficit (or a decrease in a surplus) is considered expansionary in that it tends to increase GDP ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74v04sw2dcq1ecpz3qp3rmx))
    - Note: you are spending more than you have, spending to invest as you expect more gains than what you are putting in
- A decrease in a deficit (or increase in a surplus) is considered contractionary in that it tends to decrease GDP ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74v2vvs4v3rhk3brr00dte1))
    - Note: you are saving more moeny as you no longer expect those kid off returns
- Monetary policy refers to the central bank’s actions that affect the quantity of money and credit in an economy to influence economic activity ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74v3pxrnnxdgd5g2r3f31j5))
- Monetary policy is said to be expansionary (or accommodative or easy) when the central bank increases the quantity of money and credit in an economy ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74v40hxss5xry617bhn6jcw))
    - Note: central banks do this by buying assets, thereby increasing the money flow in the economy. instead of an asset they have no introduced billions of dollars of cash in the economy, again since they dont care about interest but rather the security keeping value, allows you to make some interesting decisions
- Conversely, when the central bank is reducing the quantity of money and credit in an economy, monetary policy is said to be contractionary (or restrictive or tight). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74v4gpjee9xpqkvrz1k6e9h))
    - Note: central banks do this by selling assets thereby reducing the amount of money in the economy, as they take money out (and store it with them, but since it doesnt really matter what they do with cash as they can just print more its chill, also they dont need to earn return on that cash therefore a lot more leverage)
- Policymakers use both monetary and fiscal policies with the goals of maintaining stable prices and producing positive economic growth. Fiscal policy can also be used as a tool for redistribution of income and wealth. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74vb6te5rnxz3vtzbrrkw56))
    - Note: as reducing taxes or giving breaks where necessary
- Objectives of fiscal policy may include the following: ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74vfm5qhzg394j7xgvh0kqy))
- Influencing the level of economic activity and aggregate demand ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74vfpwxh9hvbhfk92047x8g))
    - Note: as indian government did by reducing gst, taking a hit on the income they earned, by forcing people to( indirectly to buy whatever they wished to at a time they might not have wanted to)
- Redistributing wealth and income among segments of the population ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74vjat02tg08epd2hbkdywp))
    - Note: help make poor people more well off
- Allocating resources among economic agents and sectors in the economy ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74vjpz1de42hc33ag2zkmrs))
    - Note: ensuring that some sectors, which might be taken for granted but are still important get funding and focusing on long term goals
- In general, decreased taxes and increased government spending both increase a budget deficit, overall demand, economic growth, and employment ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74vkygm13vgwfg1r19pznry))
    - Note: as decreased taxes and increased government spending are both putting money in the economy, and therefore if spent well and distributed well should increase demand, growth and employment, but also increase borrowing as government need money to borrow money to spend, and cover the lost income due to reduction in taxes
- increased taxes and decreased government spending decrease these. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k74vmwyeh7t88pv7fs4yc3k6))

- Fiscal Multiplier ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752pybxmj6s6gjkq40x22y2))
- Changes in government spending have magnified effects on aggregate demand because those whose incomes increase from increased government spending will, in turn, increase their spending, which increases the incomes and spending of others. The magnitude of the multiplier effect depends on the tax rate and on the marginal propensity to consume. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752q5tnx1mzdfm71g3b1ywq))
- To understand the calculation of the multiplier effect, consider an increase in government spending of $100 when the MPC is 80% and the tax rate is 25%. The increase in spending increases incomes by $100, but $25 (100 × 0.25) of that will be paid in taxes. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752qf2k842dqjkg4xyw5m9b))
- Disposable income is equal to income after taxes ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752qrfw48s0hb3y0yjfct9a))
- so disposable income increases by $100 × (1 − 0.25) = $75. With an MPC of 80%, additional spending by those who receive the original $100 increase is $75 × 0.8 = $60. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752qx1azmq11r5zf35bs4ry))
- This additional spending will increase others’ incomes by $60 and disposable incomes by $60 × 0.75 = $45, from which they will spend $45 × 0.8 = $36. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752r1nbdw8h2x4atperze81))
- Because each iteration of this process reduces the amount of additional spending, the effect reaches a limit. The fiscal multiplier determines the potential increase in aggregate demand resulting from an increase in government spending: ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752rdcfewxk06e9ax1z924m))
- ![](https://readwise-assets.s3.amazonaws.com/media/reader/pub/4189c58d5d7ce43aee23c58a52d5a741.png)?t=1760033531516 ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752rnjh393fb8zv8wbmznae))
- Here, with a tax rate of 25% and an MPC of 80%, the fiscal multiplier is 1 / [1 − 0.8(1 − 0.25)] = 2.5, and the increase of $100 in government spending has the potential to increase aggregate demand by $250. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752ryq2d80br011j7zs7hbg))
    - Note: funny how 100 becomes 250 eh
- The fiscal multiplier is inversely related to the tax rate (higher tax rate decreases the multiplier) and is directly related to the marginal propensity to consume (higher MPC increases the multiplier). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752sbs807tyrn2gekfbnb1s))
- Balanced Budget Multiplier ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752seq1wcxev05h2fbwhayd))
    - Note: focus on understanding the bckward logic of this section
- To balance the budget, the government could increase taxes by $100 to just offset a $100 increase in spending. Changes in taxes also have a magnified effect on aggregate demand. An increase in taxes will decrease disposable income and consumption expenditures, thereby decreasing aggregate demand. The initial decrease in spending from a tax increase of $100 is 100 × MPC = 100 × 0.8 = $80; beyond that, the multiplier effect is the same as we described for a direct increase in government spending, and the overall decrease in aggregate demand for a $100 tax increase is 100(MPC) × fiscal multiplier—or, for our example, 100(0.8)(2.5) = $200. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752svakq3dy1z53k4etagfy))
- Combining the total increase in aggregate demand from a $100 increase in government spending with the total decrease in aggregate demand from a $100 tax increase show ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752tg83jpa1sgeb8wdh0cjm))
- that the net effect on aggregate demand of both is an increase of $250 − $200 = $50, so we can say that the balanced budget multiplier is positive. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752tr1n2cdsrs9j5vsmfdns))
- If, instead of a $100 increase in taxes, we increased taxes by 100 / MPC = 100 / 0.8 = $125 and increased government spending by $100, the net effect on aggregate demand would be zero. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k752v97tx5np4jes8g1ymtgx))

- Discretionary fiscal policy is not an exact science. First, economic forecasts might be wrong, leading to incorrect policy decisions. Second, complications arise in practice that delay both the implementation of discretionary fiscal policy and the impact of policy changes on the economy. The lag between recessionary or inflationary conditions in the economy and the impact on the economy of fiscal policy changes can be divided into three types: ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k7537gdrvg1hhhfhnj8dhzxq))
- 1. Recognition lag. Discretionary fiscal policy decisions are made by a political process. The state of the economy is complex, and it may take policymakers time to recognize the nature and extent of the economic problems. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k7537mrgyt1re0mfvab6er26))
- 2. Action lag. Governments take time to discuss, vote on, and enact fiscal policy changes. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k7537pcydzx67wnxa92ph6yk))
- 3. Impact lag. Time elapses between the enactment of fiscal policy changes and when the impact of the changes on the economy actually takes place. It takes time for corporations and individuals to act on the fiscal policy changes, and fiscal multiplier effects occur only over time as well. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k7537s9w4szh75qkk52g4et6))
- t ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k7539hdjqn71ass5yhs6cr39))
- These lags can actually make fiscal policy counterproductive. For example, if the economy is in a recession phase, fiscal stimulus may be deemed appropriate. However, by the time fiscal stimulus is implemented and has its full impact, the economy may already be on a path to a recovery driven by the private sector. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k753820bffwx992sbfqv32pv))

- Additional macroeconomic issues may hinder the usefulness of fiscal policy: Misreading economic statistics. The full employment level for an economy is not precisely measurable. If the government relies on expansionary fiscal policy mistakenly at a time when the economy is already at full capacity, it will simply drive inflation higher. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k753a51myca837jfytyavyqy))
- Crowding-out effect. Expansionary fiscal policy may crowd out private investment. Greater government borrowing tends to increase interest rates, which decreases private investments (some investments will be uneconomic with higher borrowing costs). This crowding-out effect can reduce the impact of expansionary fiscal policy on aggregate demand. Opinions vary on the magnitude of this effect. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k753a98fdcwgq0gn9zbts4hb))
- Supply shortages. If economic activity is slow due to resource constraints (low availability of labor or other resources) and not due to low demand, expansionary fiscal policy will fail to achieve its objective and will probably lead to higher inflation. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k753am4a1q1qca6khtj628wn))
- Limits to deficits. There is a limit to expansionary fiscal policy. If the markets perceive that the deficit is already too high as a proportion of GDP, funding the deficit will be problematic. - Monetary policy is implemented using the monetary policy tools of the central bank. The three main policy tools of central banks are as follows: ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790ejqxbwp2cqn6jwe8xszy))
- 1. Policy rate. In the United States, banks can borrow funds from the Fed if they have temporary shortfalls in reserves ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790ffx76aake04akv5wqacs))
- The rate at which banks can borrow reserves from the Fed is called the discount rate ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790fs93rg8e7excykkk9jpw))
- For the European Central Bank (ECB), it is called the refinancing rate. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790fw4k35b97kps73bm2fbe))
- One way to lend money to banks is through a repurchase agreement. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790hjt9qxrfygvnah6srw8j))
- The central bank purchases securities from banks that, in turn, agree to repurchase the securities at a higher price in the future. The percentage difference between the purchase price and the repurchase price is effectively the rate at which the central bank is lending to member banks. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790ht8k55syqyhwvmpvfy3v))
- The Bank of England uses this method, and its policy rate is called the two-week repo (repurchase) rate. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790j1pr3d14rk81vvg7ehwc))
- A lower rate reduces banks’ cost of funds, ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790j44762qrkrgnvk089xv3))
- encourages lending, and tends to decrease interest rates overall. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790j76mnvstb26f2pcp92z1))
- A higher policy rate has the opposite effect, decreasing lending and increasing interest rates. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790jas20rckb00k5pp9ctjb))
- In the United States, the federal funds rate is the rate that banks charge each other on overnight loans of reserves. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790ktan2grk3pc9xen4pyf7))
- The Fed sets a target for this market-determined rate and uses open market operations to influence it toward the target rate ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790m67z523xf16e4q6v7jyk))
- 2. Reserve requirements. By increasing the reserve requirement (the percentage of deposits that banks are required to retain as reserves), the central bank effectively decreases the funds that are available for lending and thereby decreases the money supply, which will tend to increase interest rates ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790nxrv1e3kv6danka9rxgc))
    - Note: Increasing the reserve requirement, allows central banks to take money out of the economy
- . A decrease in the reserve requirement will increase the funds available for lending and the money supply, which will tend to decrease interest rates. This tool only works well to increase the money supply if banks are willing to lend and customers are willing to borrow ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790q45z0687kjvyzh3x1aqb))
- 3. Open market operations. Buying and selling of securities by the central bank is referred to as open market operations ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790rbd9339qf9zkqdqxp3q5))
- When the central bank buys securities, cash replaces securities in investor accounts, banks have excess reserves, more funds are available for lending, the money supply increases, and interest rates decrease ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790rpp6k7mtv8y6vb7b82jc))
- Sales of securities by the central bank have the opposite effect, reducing cash in investor accounts, excess reserves, funds available for lending, and the money supply, which will tend to cause interest rates to increase ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790s0e0z0d208v483cxdda9))

- In the United States, open market operations are the Fed’s most commonly used tool and are important in achieving the federal funds target rate. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k790v9ge37f3jrtr139e340k))
    - Note: they influence the rate using open market operations, by actively buying and selling assets

- Some countries, especially developing countries, use exchange rate targeting ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k798xzavc1znyw25swff9w8x))
- That is, they target a foreign exchange rate between their currency and another (often the U.S.
  dollar), rather than targeting inflation. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k798y3p8kxtfzsh1w5x1x9g0))
- , consider a country that has targeted an exchange rate for its currency versus the U.S. dollar. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k798yjfcgzbe2wcvmw0psbkj))
- If the foreign exchange value of the domestic currency falls relative to the U.S. dollar, the monetary authority must use foreign reserves to purchase their domestic currency (which will reduce money supply growth and increase interest rates) to reach the target exchange rate ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k798yxmnh19ygqwgrqxwggbx))
- an increase in the foreign exchange value of the domestic currency above the target rate will require sale of the domestic currency in currency markets to reduce ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k798z2jxjh1xf8843w17jwnh))
- its value (increasing the domestic money supply and decreasing interest rates) to move toward the target exchange rate ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k798z93zc4jtkh2kb9w2y6p0))
- One result of exchange rate targeting may be greater volatility of the money supply because domestic monetary policy must adapt to the necessity of maintaining a stable foreign exchange rate. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k798zf80qmq7xh3cwmd23h2e))
- Over the short term, the targeting country can purchase or sell its currency in the foreign exchange markets to influence the exchange rate. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k798zt5rn5vshnssqc8mvfbd))
- There are limits, however, on how much influence currency purchases or sales can have on exchange rates over time. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k798zz3cqrjcrezvkfpbreas))
- For example, a country may run out of foreign reserves with which to purchase its currency when the exchange value of its currency is still below the target exchange rate. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k79908qney4baevgccygt2z1))
- The net effect of exchange rate targeting is that the targeting country will have the same inflation rate as the country with the targeted currency ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k7993dd9vt6kjyh2aq73k9bd))
- and the targeting country will need to follow monetary policy and accept interest rates that are consistent with this goal regardless of domestic economic circumstances. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k7993jdrm4fwgspcqszvd1cy))

- Conversely, increasing the money supply to stimulate economic activity could lead to an increase in expected inflation rates and long-term bond yields, even as short-term rates fall ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k79cywhxr93ymgkvt9gf2va7))

- Such a situation is called a liquidity trap. - The World Bank is a vital source of financial and technical assistance to developing countries around the world. Our mission is to fight poverty with passion and professionalism for lasting results and to help people help themselves and their environment by providing resources, sharing knowledge, building capacity, and forging partnerships in the public and private sectors.
  We are not a bank in the common sense; we are made up of two unique development institutions owned by 187 member countries: the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development (IBRD) and the International Development Association (IDA).
  Each institution plays a different but collaborative role in advancing the vision of inclusive and sustainable globalization. The IBRD aims to reduce poverty in middle-income and creditworthy poorer countries, while IDA focuses on the world’s poorest countries.
  […] Together, we provide low-interest loans, interest-free credits, and grants to developing countries for a wide array of purposes that include investments in education, health, public administration, infrastructure, financial and private sector development, agriculture, and environmental and natural resource management. - The following is according to the World Trade Organization (WTO) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k7a11vpgyyyankwar267224k))
- The World Trade Organization (WTO) is the only international organization dealing with the global rules of trade between nations. Its main function is to ensure that trade flows as smoothly, predictably, and freely as possible.
  […] Trade friction is channeled into the WTO’s dispute settlement process where the focus is on interpreting agreements and commitments, and how to ensure that countries’ trade policies conform with them. That way, the risk of disputes spilling over into political or military conflict is reduced.
  […] At the heart of the system—known as the multilateral trading system—are the WTO’s agreements, negotiated and signed by a large majority of the world’s trading nations, and ratified in their parliaments. These agreements are the legal ground-rules for international commerce.
  Essentially, they are contracts, guaranteeing member countries important trade rights. They also bind governments to keep their trade policies within agreed limits to everybody’s benefit. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01k7a11zt6ad9yfcsxb2bqtfzf))

- When we speak of the gains from trade in general, it is based on economic analysis suggesting that, overall, the gains from trade are greater than the losses—especially in the long run. That is, the gainers could, at least in theory, compensate the losers, with net gains shared by both importing and exporting countries. While free trade surely imposes costs on the workers and companies in an industry facing competition from imports in the short run, it is argued that in the long run—after workers receive additional training and find work in other industries—the short-run costs of free trade are mitigated to some significant degree, or even reversed. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01ka3medd1ffs6yzkxdstwhpa9))

- A tariff placed on an imported good increases its domestic price, decreases the quantity imported, and increases the quantity supplied domestically.     - Note: assuming the company can get domestic production up quickly enough, otherwise in the SR the government might face slower growth
- A quota restricts the quantity of a good imported to the quota amount. Domestic producers gain, and domestic consumers lose from an increase in the domestic price.
  The right to export a specific quantity to the domestic country is granted by the domestic government, which may or may not charge for the import licenses to foreign countries. If the import licenses are sold, the domestic government gains the revenue. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01ka3p812443c3wwayj59bengm))
- We define a quota that is equivalent to a given tariff as a quota that will result in the same decrease in the quantity of a good imported as the tariff. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01ka3q8f9d715bvye0fxqfcxad))
- At Pworld, before any restriction, the domestic quantity supplied is QS1 ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01ka3qb2kf45rtxdnjnb2n5w5e))
- domestic quantity demanded is QD1 ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01ka3qb6mc26ba8zq7zm91e7k9))
- the difference equal to the quantity imported, QD1 − QS1 ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01ka3qbbehwfft6yfkns2pv51p))
- Placing a tariff on imports increases the domestic price to Pprotection, ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01ka3r57pemp4v50neaccxym6j))
- increases the domestic quantity supplied to QS2, ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01ka3r5ax3wztyw5xtgqp1ces9))
- decreases the domestic quantity demanded to QD2 ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01ka3r5fh0pzy1bfbv96w3ktvc))
- The difference is the new quantity imported. An equivalent quota will have the same effect, decreasing the quantity imported to QD2 − QS2. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01ka3r8z3kqd4r4a8mcw928kn9))

- In terms of overall economic gains from trade, the deadweight loss is the amount of lost welfare from the imposition of a quota or tariff. - If none of the quota rents are captured by the domestic government, the overall welfare loss to the domestic economy is greater by the amount of the quota rents. - VERs are another way of protecting the domestic producers in the importing country. - Corporations regularly engage in cross-border transactions, purchase and sell foreign currencies as a result, and enter into FX forward contracts to hedge the risk of expected future receipts and payments denominated in foreign currencies. - Leveraged accounts refer to the various types of investment firms that use derivatives, including hedge funds, firms that trade for their own accounts, and other trading firms of various types. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfpw8g1fbja9yfxqq26dsqp))
    - Note: leveraged accounts are used by institutions that make bets on leveraged “loan” money. due to the their high short term conviction, high risk tolerance, and lack of capital they often need to take loans to hold certain positions. 
      Mutual funds, have a higher capital base, due to their larger AUM, more rules and lower risk tolerance only trade on their own assets under management. they trade with money that is theirs and dont take loans to pay for positions. due to this often trade for the LR, which allows them to reduce their risk profile and to some extent have more guaranteed returns that is required for the type of funds they are given. 
      hedge funds are highly specialised investments types not open to the general public or rarely available to the general public, while mutual funds are generally open to everyone.

- An exchange rate is simply the price or cost of units of one currency in terms of another. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfqa27f1f6098t3m5xfjg8a))
- These are often referred to as the base currency and the price currency ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfqmjbpgwpwzsrcesvx9rrb))
- In a foreign currency quotation, we have the price of one currency in units of another currency. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfqmmmmy3n8agve3m02zvxp))
- In the quotation 1.25 USD/EUR, the USD is the price currency and the EUR is the base currency ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfqmvjf3hq0e1sq6yj5k03m))
- The price of one euro (base currency) is 1.25 USD (the price currency), so 1.25 is the price of one unit of the base currency in terms of the other ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfqnq347wbnejrasvsk1721))
- An exchange rate expressed as price currency/base currency is referred to as a direct quote from the point of view of an investor in the price currency country and an indirect quote from the point of view of an investor in the base currency country ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfqsatdsjqyvhqj67zg0dg3))
- . For example, a quote of 1.17 USD/EUR would be a direct quote for a USD-based investor and an indirect quote for a EUR-based investor. Conversely, a quote of 1 / 1.17 = 0.855 EUR/USD would be a direct quote for a EUR-based investor and an indirect quote for a USD-based investor. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfqt5sp4vwd4z9qgfn0amgh))
- The exchange rate at a point in time is referred to as a nominal exchange rate. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfqtm2tk8st1pmq97mx6vqy))
- If this rate (price/base) increases, the cost of a unit of the base currency in terms of the price currency has increased, so that the purchasing power of the price currency has decreased ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfqtzdbgtynbfzbtdhmdfbb))
    - Note: that is u need to now spend more money in price currency terms to get a single unit of the base currency. since the base currency hasnt changed, the price currecny has devalued
- If the USD/EUR exchange rate increases from 1.10 to 1.15, the cost of 100 euros increases from $110 to $115. The purchasing power of the dollar has decreased relative to the euro because the cost of 100 euros’ worth of goods to a consumer in the United States has increased over the period. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfqxfq30mzd0cbv5e3xp7k1))
- The real exchange rate between two currencies refers to the purchasing power of one currency in terms of the amount of goods priced in another currency, relative to an earlier (base) period. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfx44my90wmec62dmkwfqr4))
    - Note: the real exchange rate here is meant to be understood as, the rate between 2 currencies, and the purchasing power of one currency in terms of (relative to) amount of good you can buy in another currency, that is again relative an earlier base period
- Consider a situation in which the nominal USD/EUR exchange rate is unchanged at 1.00 over a period and the price level in the United States is unchanged, while prices in the Eurozone have increased by 5%. Eurozone goods that cost 100 euros at the beginning of the period cost 105 euros at the end of the period. With the nominal exchange rate unchanged, the purchasing power of the USD in the Eurozone has decreased, because exchanging 100 USD for 100 EUR will now buy only 100 / 105 = 95.2% of the goods 100 EUR could buy at the beginning of the period. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfxa0j4w1v2vwe6jas3s0yn))
- Here is a summary: An increase in the nominal USD/EUR rate decreases the purchasing power of the USD in the Eurozone (and increases the purchasing power of the EUR in the United States); the real USD/EUR exchange rate has increased. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfxcggw630epeq9dfty8wmg))
    - Note: x usd/eur, here x increases, that is what is meant by nominal usd/eur rate increasing, therefor eyou buy 1 euro for more usd, therefore you need to pay more usd for each euro and therefore the usd has devalued.
- A decrease in the nominal USD/EUR rate increases the purchasing power of the USD in the Eurozone (and decreases the purchasing power of the EUR in the United States); the real USD/EUR exchange rate has decreased. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfxt78e9ds8qpz40yvm4pav))
    - Note: that means the amount of usd u need to pay for a euro has decreased. therefore euro has devalued in comparison to the dollar
- An increase in the Eurozone price level, relative to the price level in the United States, will increase the real USD/EUR exchange rate, decreasing the purchasing power of the USD in the Eurozone (and increasing the purchasing power of the EUR in the United States). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfxvtab6nq4nee4g7ss9g5w))
- A decrease in the Eurozone price level, relative to the price level in the United States, will decrease the real USD/EUR exchange rate, increasing the purchasing power of the USD in the Eurozone (and decreasing the purchasing power of the EUR in the United States). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfxw21g5wg2f7t5528k812a))
- ![](https://readwise-assets.s3.amazonaws.com/media/reader/pub/1534148b4c7da901b2d6b3b59f0d1eca.png) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfxwx23hth97k0nc73w63da))
- An increase (decrease) in the nominal exchange rate over the period increases (decreases) the end-of period real exchange rate, and the purchasing power of the price currency decreases (increases). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfxytdqja2vpsbj4rdqsy4c))
    - Note: increase in nominal will increase real and decrease the purchasing power. that means price currency has devalued relative to the book. 
      decrease in nominal will decrease real and increase the purchasing power. book currency has devalued relative to the price
- An increase in the price level in the price currency country relative to the price level in the base currency country will decrease the real exchange rate, increasing the purchasing power of the price currency in terms of base country goods. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfy1g9k2k220f9ysz0afdfv))
    - Note: increasde in price decreases real and decreases nominal and increases purchasing power relative to the book as for x euros here you can only buy a %of x in dollars due to increase in price. while x dollars buys x+x%in euros
- Conversely, a decrease in the price level in the price currency country relative to the price level in the base currency country will increase the real exchange rate, decreasing the purchasing power of the price currency in terms of base country goods. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbfy62tdxm7rj7dm9gx9m5tc))
- A spot exchange rate is the currency exchange rate for immediate delivery, which for most currencies means the exchange of currencies takes place two days after the trade. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbg076ddxyc8v2ptaqjge53z))
    - Note: that means although u have decided that this is the pripce we will trade on, the currency itself changes hands only 2 days after the trade takes place
- A forward exchange rate is a currency exchange rate for an exchange to be done in the future. Forward rates are quoted for various future dates (e.g., 30 days, 60 days, 90 days, or one year). A forward is actually an agreement to exchange a specific amount of one currency for a specific amount of another on a future date specified in the forward agreement. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbg0bs4p8v6se7cqes8q4hta))
- A French firm that will receive 10 million GBP from a British firm six months from now has uncertainty about the amount of euros that payment will be equivalent to six months from now. By entering into a forward agreement covering 10 million GBP at the 6-month forward rate of 1.192 EUR/GBP, the French firm has agreed to exchange 10 million GBP for 11.92 million euros in six months. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbvprmrb2ejzgz2wh77x07n9))
- Consider a USD/EUR exchange rate that has changed from 1.42 to 1.39 USD/EUR. The percentage change in the dollar price of a euro is simply 1.39 / 1.42 − 1 = −0.0211 = −2.11%. Because the dollar price of a euro has fallen, the euro has depreciated relative to the dollar, and a euro now buys 2.11% fewer U.S. dollars. It is correct to say that the euro has depreciated by 2.11% relative to the dollar. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbvpssffjevhhs22335953k2))
- On the other hand, it is not correct to say that the dollar has appreciated by 2.11%. To calculate the percentage appreciation of the dollar, we need to convert the quotes to EUR/USD. So, our beginning quote of 1.42 USD/EUR becomes 1 / 1.42 = 0.7042 EUR/USD, and our ending quote of 1.39 USD/EUR becomes 1 / 1.39 = 0.7194 EUR/USD.
  Using these exchange rates, we can calculate the change in the euro price of a dollar as 0.7194 / 0.7042 − 1 = 0.0216 = 2.16%. In this case, it is correct to say that the dollar has appreciated 2.16% with respect to the euro ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbvptck937q82cpfv6h7ew7d))
