- A country can be a member of a monetary union in which several countries use a common currency. Within the European Union, for example, most countries use the euro. While individual countries give up the ability to set domestic monetary policy, they all participate in determining the monetary policy of the European Central Bank. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbyp8e02tyb6487kqjvvd848))
- Countries That Have Their Own Currency ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbyp8hgf8ewpgamcj3db4m49))
- A currency board arrangement is an explicit commitment to exchange domestic currency for a specified foreign currency at a fixed exchange rate ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbyp9sxtea9dhes6wjs5dn1h))
- In Hong Kong, currency is (and may be) only issued when fully backed by holdings of an equivalent amount of U.S. dollars. The Hong Kong Monetary Authority can earn interest on its U.S. dollar balances. With dollarization, there is no such income, as the income is earned by the U.S. Federal Reserve when it buys interest-bearing assets with the U.S. currency it issues ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbypaw5f949xbzvk9tyk2g6g))
- While the monetary authority gives up the ability to conduct independent monetary policy and essentially imports the inflation rate of the outside currency, there may be some latitude to affect interest rates over the short term. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbytmcbpwyrse7493sc0rtnf))
- In a conventional fixed peg arrangement, a country pegs its currency within margins of ±1% versus another currency or a basket that includes the currencies of its major trading or financial partners. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbytmnb97tcs8m5jah2qgygy))
- The monetary authority can maintain exchange rates within the band by purchasing or selling foreign currencies in the foreign exchange markets (direct intervention) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbytmvzgw8wbzrkq9e08ah44))
- In addition, the country can use indirect intervention, including changes in interest rate policy, regulation of foreign exchange transactions, and convincing people to constrain foreign exchange activity ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbytn5hthg24zc7mw2dbrnz3))
- The monetary authority retains more flexibility to conduct monetary policy than with dollarization, a monetary union, or a currency board. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbyvk37gbs7k7tr2w0a74s6n))
- In a system of pegged exchange rates within horizontal bands or a target zone, the permitted fluctuations in currency value relative to another currency or basket of currencies are wider (e.g., ±2%) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbywvfskfz7pbbnvx42n6zy9))
- Compared to a conventional peg, the monetary authority has more policy discretion because the bands are wider. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbyxanny1bf2nkdesj3jmyk3))
- With a crawling peg, the exchange rate is adjusted periodically, typically to adjust for higher inflation versus the currency used in the peg ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbz3baz8q2mc021cwf2j9cf3))
- This is termed a passive crawling peg as opposed to an active crawling peg, in which a series of exchange rate adjustments over time is announced and implemented. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbz3bk75w0e7kgbp9pb0kg19))
- An active crawling peg can influence inflation expectations, adding some predictability to domestic inflation. Monetary policy is restricted in much the same way it is with a fixed peg arrangement. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbz3bxetvr88gc7670944qqh))
- With management of exchange rates within crawling bands, the width of the bands that identify permissible exchange rates is increased over time. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kbz3x9x894g4791xabjwx2we))

- A cross rate is the exchange rate between two currencies implied by their exchange rates with a common third currency ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc18w7gtb8wb5zt4w3ftspmv))
- Cross-rates are necessary when there is no active FX market in a currency pair ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc190545gw9dww1eshew18y6))
- The rate must be computed from the exchange rates between each of these two currencies and a third currency, usually the USD or EUR ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc19088pya8gy0h8vccx12ad))
- When currencies are freely traded and forward currency contracts exist, the percentage difference between forward and spot exchange rates is approximately equal to the difference between the two countries’ interest rates ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc19wzed6qgw5wx6n9nsk15p))
- This is because there is an arbitrage trade with a riskless profit to be made when this relation does not hold ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc19y5hdf9xeta912hyq6fqd))
- We call this a no-arbitrage condition because if it doesn’t hold, there is an opportunity to make a profit without risk ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc1af92zxpf2vfjvgdf2xgvy))
- The possible arbitrage is as follows: borrow Currency A at Interest Rate A, convert it to Currency B at the spot rate and invest it to earn Interest Rate B, and sell the proceeds from this investment forward at the forward rate to turn it back into Currency A. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc1b25e4q4gfv76ths23adfe))
    - Note: as in you borrow currency A, at interest rate A, convert it into B and earn interest B. Therefore the difference between the interest you earn on B - A is your profit and then you just sell the currency as the difference is arbitraged away
- If the forward rate does not correctly reflect the difference between interest rates, such an arbitrage could generate a profit to the extent that the return from investing Currency B and converting it back to Currency A with a forward contract is greater than the cost of borrowing Currency A for the period. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc1d44d7qhy94t8c40bnxarq))
    - Note: the above option was fpor spot rates and spot rating arbitrage, what if we wnat to use forward rates and find the arbitrage for the forward rate. so we can just take currency B invest it and the return you get is greater when converted than borrowing currency A
- The no-arbitrage condition requires that “you cannot earn more than your domestic riskless rate of interest by borrowing your domestic currency, converting it to a foreign currency to invest at the foreign riskless rate, and exchanging back to your domestic currency.” ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc1hyb2fd22ybh85ex7x37by))

- READING 20 ORGANIZATIONAL FORMS, CORPORATE ISSUER FEATURES, AND OWNERSHIP ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3cc6430fbdkxqdw3e0yf64))
- MODULE 20.1: FEATURES OF CORPORATE ISSUERS ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3ckvf3gtp8a1mtmh5e4ctf))
- Organizational forms refer to how businesses are set up from a legal and organizational point of view. Key features of organizational forms include the following: ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3cswy5xz560qr684qe7nxx))
- Whether the business is a separate legal entity from the owner(s) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3cszcaf118n5en6xzwm8n4))
- Whether the owners of the business also operate the business, and if not, the nature of the relationship between its owners and operators ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3cw4ks2haj5623czm1ga52))
- Whether the owners’ liability for the actions and debts of the business is limited or unlimited ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3cwncpc8td44727er0s4zq))
- The tax treatment of profits or losses from the business ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3cws3z42gvc2eha6wnzrp7))
- Access to additional capital to fund expansion and to distribute risk ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3cwxkcfh7rwmszzwpr6361))
- A sole proprietorship is a business owned and operated by an individual ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3cx74ac4887wr227jxbhg6))
- Legally, the business is an extension of the owner, who is personally responsible for claims against the business (i.e., unlimited liability) and receives all profits/losses. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3d4yyexrmvy7wmmk62y7an))
- Profits are then taxed as personal income of the owner ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3d53tmxv75qvnwdrff5yft))
- Sole proprietorships tend to be small in scale because they can only expand within the limits of the individual owner’s ability to secure financing. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3d5hygb79vv5jfwv2fakt7))
- To do business on a scale that exceeds that of a sole proprietorship, two or more individuals can form a general partnership ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3d6gmm3w50m9kczkvhp15d))
- In this structure, the partnership agreement specifies each partner’s responsibilities for business operations and their shares of the partnership profits or losses ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3d6nzbx38zevgn1fx5my9b))
- The agreement may be written, verbal, or even incidental through the actions of the partners ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3d6v9qyr3wggx9vd0d2fsp))
- As with a sole proprietorship, the ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3d6y88j1kmv28qjk56r2cj))
- partners have unlimited liability for claims against the business, and profits from the business allocated to each partner are taxed as personal income ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3d72vs5qrcr3as7ygyv8r3))
- A limited partnership involves two levels of partners. One or more general partners operate the business and have unlimited liability, as in a general partnership, but this structure also has limited partners who are liable only for the amount they invest in the partnership (i.e., limited liability) and have claims to its profits that are proportionate to their investments. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3d8y36qqde9yy8d94ej7xz))
    - Note: the general partners, have unlimited liability but they also operate the business. the limited partners have limited liability, and they dont operate the business and are only liable for the amount of money they have invested, so the maximum they can lose is only their amount they have invested.
- Limited partners typically are not involved in appointing or removing general partners. How the profits are divided among the general and limited partners is specified in the partnership agreement ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3djhpq7crwrkt5m4h6p1yb))
- Because they are responsible for managing the business, the general partners typically receive a larger portion of profits than the limited partners. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3djr3mf4rye61wmtcja3e6))
- Profits allocated to the partners are taxed as personal income to each partner ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3dkdstq7fw0s351cte12pv))
- Some jurisdictions allow a limited liability partnership (LLP) wherein a general partner is not required, and all the partners are limited partners. In the United States, LLPs are only allowed for providers of professional services such as law, accounting, and so on, and there are restrictions on the number of partners and the amount of equity investment. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3dnmcvp1k4x3d8xx6g1szb))
- The feature that distinguishes a corporation, or limited company, from the other business structures is that a corporation is a legal entity separate from its owners and managers ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3dwm78mrh4yk255n15v3s4))
- In this case, all of the corporation’s shareholders have limited liability ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3e1s0xx8yj1neck8q734qa))
- An owner can lose his entire investment if the company goes bankrupt and the value of his shares goes to zero. Beyond their initial investments, shareholders are not responsible for claims against the corporation. A corporation may, but is not required to, distribute its profits to its owners. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3e3t6vy9erbv9fza3c8k4t))
- Most large firms are corporations because that structure gives them the greatest access to capital, both debt (borrowed capital) and equity (ownership capital). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3e3wdysbr9smkwfy2vxt3y))
- Another distinguishing feature of corporations is the separation of its owners and managers. An investor who buys shares of a corporation does not directly influence the company’s day-to-day operations. Instead, the owners appoint a board of directors that is responsible for hiring the senior managers to operate the company. The board and the managers it hires are responsible for acting in the interests of the shareholders. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3ec3h8yyxbxmj5q75mtgdm))
- A public corporation (or a public limited company) is one that has shares that are sold to the public and trade in an organized market (stock exchange) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3efcyq86h93f7kr7a71tz1))
- A private limited company is similar to a public company, but it has a limited number of shareholders and restrictions on transfer of shares. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3efkgg1tqa895zmrkhktf0))
- Depending on the country, a corporation’s profits may be subject to double taxation if the government taxes companies on their earnings and it taxes dividends (which are distributions of earnings to owners) as personal income. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc3eg2yt3m28y15vdyv816yg))
    - Note: double taxation in this case on the companies earning and on the dividends
- A corporation’s legal identity is separate from that of its owners and is formed by filing an articles of incorporation with a regulatory body ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc6jg65p4jtygwxv13z0vyj1))
- . As a legal entity, a corporation has many of the rights and responsibilities of an individual, such as the right to hire employees, enter into contracts, borrow and lend money, and so on. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc6k1t4qypt3rtwpzej6mpp6))
- A corporation issues shares to the owners (shareholders), which allows it to raise large amounts of capital. Shareholders have voting rights that allow them to elect the board of directors ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc6khggsq3vyrnagtck3nc1a))
- Shares are easily transferable if they are traded on an exchange. The board of directors may distribute a portion of the company’s earnings to the shareholders as dividends ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc6khrxt6ykxtjkg5pjejae8))
- The disadvantage of double taxation of corporate income is less for investors in companies that pay out a smaller fraction of their profits in dividends and reinvest the remaining, ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc6p05swy4wn57avg83619wn))
- Most public limited companies are listed companies, which means their shares are listed on an exchange, allowing investors to trade shares. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc6wxs1f3m8a8t7z3541c0ts))
- A stock exchange is a rules- based open market, providing price and volume transparency. Shareholders in a company can be individuals, other corporations, nonprofits, or government ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc6x9c0gs04jzgp6c1jsg63s))
- Shares that are actively traded (i.e., not held by insiders, strategic investors, or sponsors) are called the company’s free float. Free float is typically expressed as a percentage of total outstanding shares. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc6x9jsfagkhhz46ekcnwdbc))
    - Note: that is stocks that are currently being traded
- Public companies are subject to compliance and reporting requirements. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc71rxdxjq2z7dswg8y9xnqy))
- companies are required to file quarterly or annual financial reports with a regulatory body, and to disclose any material changes in the company’s business or ownership. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc71s5xdkrf78q80zs5n5sne))
- Shares in private limited companies do not trade on an exchange. As a result, their value is not readily observable, and transfer between investors is difficult. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc71scesdzznfmnm3baapxry))
- investors in a private limited company have to wait until the company goes public or is sold to exit their investment. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc71sjvkh2ct91628dsab8sf))
- Private companies have fewer regulatory requirements and typically disclose less information than public companies. Also, with fewer investors, private companies can take a longer-term view of the business. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc71ssa3g7kkymax9yrr82jg))
- Private companies can raise equity capital through private placements of securities. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc71whe3pvwapr1w0acjrfrt))
- Private placements are typically restricted to accredited investors such as corporate and institutional investors or high net worth individuals. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc71wsynddtbmq4zh9jywdck))
- A company can become public by issuing shares in an initial public offering (IPO). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc725ttwtdyrwwx2g4t7hfmc))
- To conduct an IPO, the company must meet exchange-specific requirements. Companies typically engage the services of an investment bank to underwrite the issue. Once the shares are listed on an exchange, owners can sell shares, and new owners can buy shares, without dealing directly with the company. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc726hwfrt3ny7ed54v6r9wf))
- In a direct listing, a stock exchange agrees to list a private company’s existing shares. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc727y4fxhc0r0f7k1pm6x8c))
- This differs from an IPO in that a direct listing does not raise any new capital for the company, but it has advantages in that it can be done more quickly than an IPO and without involving an underwriter. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc7282kmt91mdbd0dg3nsh8h))
    - Note: no investment bank taking a piece of the pie but no new capital either
- A special purpose acquisition company (SPAC) is a corporate structure set up to acquire a private company in the future. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc72ezt71m0ty3ex5ab999k9))
- The SPAC raises capital through an IPO and puts the funds into a trust that it must use to make an acquisition within a specified time. The acquired company does not have to be identified at the time of the IPO. For this reason, SPACs are also known as “blank check” companies. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc72h5w90b06brmch3c2swne))
- Sometimes there can be a benefit to taking an underperforming public company private to restructure it and unlock its potential value. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc72n7gh69jtzj0ks8959hvj))
- In such a case, an acquirer purchases all the outstanding shares of a public company, and the company is delisted from the exchange. The benefits of going private include a lower regulatory burden and the associated cost savings. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc72nctyx090nsebmqha4zxy))
    - Note: how private equity works essentially
- A. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc72pezjpvajajd7vvf9d160))
- B. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc72rwe370wst3dwrwq7byfx))

- READING 21 INVESTORS AND OTHER STAKEHOLDERS ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc72vhgdbfygf6k40yrbs0hh))
- MODULE 21.1: STAKEHOLDERS AND ESG FACTORS ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc72wg7yz3mz8njt6qt3tj1x))
- A company’s lenders (debtholders) have a legal, contractual claim to the interest and principal payments the company has promised to make. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc730pq2265n6bknp9gvfpt3))
- Owners (equity holders) have a residual claim to the company’s net assets (i.e., what remains after all other claims have been paid) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc730x093ngcfg2gj2nc9gb7))
- That is, lenders have a higher priority of claims than equity owners. Because debt is less risky than equity, it is a less costly form of capital. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc73121hwj4m9aphtcdkx1ja))
- Both debtholders and equity holders can potentially lose their entire investment if a company fails, but their losses cannot exceed the amounts they have invested. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc733fwmm5f9t3gmnngbcpnh))
- A key difference between debt and equity investments is their upside potential. Regardless of a company’s success, the best result debtholders can achieve is to receive the interest and principal payments promised by the company. Equity, on the other hand, has a theoretically unlimited upside if a company succeeds and grows over time. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc74k008fsm60370m3ctmy87))
- The value of a company is the sum of the value of its debt and the value of its equity. As the company’s value increases (assuming its value is greater than the value of its debt), the value of equity increases with it, while the value of debt is constant (i.e., no upside for debt investors from company growth). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc76fvjx9xejrrq422c57ykx))
- If the value of a company is less than the value of its debt, the value of its equity is zero, and the value of its debt is below the promised amount and moves with the value of the company (i.e., debt has downside exposure). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc76g2a3gdk59vj7dwz0mpag))
- As can be seen in the previous example, increasing leverage (and therefore, risk) can increase the return on equity as long as the expected rate of return on assets exceeds the cost of debt. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc901g8j2qw8366861gn6sg7))
    - Note: emphasis on the as long as the expected rate of return on assets exeeds the cost of debt
- If things work out, and the actual return on assets is equal to or exceeds the cost of debt, the equity investors benefit from taking the risk. Debt investors, however, receive only their promised 10% interest (and the return of principal when the debt matures). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc90cp4jgf4pmhbfr01hrv5t))
- Assuming no change in cash operating expenses, a 15% decrease in revenues would reduce the ROE to 5% (100% equity scenario) or to 0% (50/50 debt and equity scenario). If the decrease in revenues exceeded 15%, revenues would not have been enough to cover operating expenses, and debt investors would receive less than their promised 10% interest. Again, debt investors have no upside—only potential downside. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc90k4k0v2xjxtne3wb39pq6))
- Because of the difference in their risk profiles, the interests of debtholders may conflict with the interests of equity holders. Debtholders are primarily concerned with a company’s ability to repay its obligations and less concerned with its growth prospects ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc911thhjmdaw92abqsf9j3c))
- Equity holders may favor actions that increase a company’s potential growth, but also increase its risk level, such as adding financial leverage by issuing new debt ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc912w4j1ya84ja840w95sgy))
- Issuing ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc912xzz2vvb89210tww9j9b))
- additional debt as opposed to equity also prevents dilution of shareholders’ proportional ownership ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc9135ys3tbenc24yx7y7pxh))
- A company’s existing debtholders may oppose such actions because increasing the company’s risk (and the probability of defaulting on its debts) does not increase their expected return. Therefore, debt investors usually limit borrowers’ actions by including contractual provisions such as maximum leverage or a minimum interest coverage ratio (known as covenants, which we will examine in the Fixed Income topic area) in debt agreements. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc914cywgmeabgq7yhcm4dhb))
- Under shareholder theory, the primary focus of corporate governance is the interests of the firm’s shareholders, which is to maximize the market value of the firm’s common equity ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc914s7mtpw6s28fh7pfnx8y))
- Under this theory, corporate governance is primarily concerned with the conflict of interest between the firm’s managers and its owners (shareholders).
  The focus of corporate governance under stakeholder theory is broader. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc917z4g4kqkytf7ndnwb096))
- It considers conflicts among several groups that have an interest in the activities and performance of the firm. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc9187dw2kxejjz5307yjte7))
- Stakeholders of a Corporation ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc918axdngvmhh39xs32q9qj))
- Shareholders have a residual interest in the corporation, in that they have claim to the net assets of the corporation after all liabilities have been settled ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc91c4yqnb2drvgcakf1advt))
- Shareholders have voting rights to elect the board of directors and for other important corporate matters, which gives them control over the firm and its management ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc91cbzh5t1qsa5esq6zrkm4))
- They have an interest in ongoing profitability and growth of the firm that will increase the value of their ownership shares. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc91cfy38w0w2dg5fhm68h0k))
- Lenders can be public or private debtholders ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc91f7spy8w3ptpgpycxsz4k))
- Public debtholders or bondholders supply debt capital to the firm and are primarily owners of the firm’s outstanding bonds, ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc91fq8c29sdy9tt1t5wfvfx))
    - Note: public debtholders or bondholders are people who buy bonds issued by a company or a country and then when you buy these bonds, the company gets the value loaned. biggest thing here is that these bonds can be traded
- while private debtholders such as banks extend loans, credit facilities, and leases to the firm. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc91ftegdt7s1jtp59aw7e3e))
    - Note: private debtholders are banks and institutions that just lend money their money lent cannot be traded.
- Private debtholders may have access to nonpublic information from company management, which decreases information asymmetry. For this reason, private debtholders are a critical source of financing for small-to-medium-sized businesses. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc96dj176a7759ph98xmq3hr))
- . In some cases, private debtholders hold equity in the firm, allowing them to take a more equity-like approach to evaluating the company and potentially making them more amenable to changes in terms of the loans (i.e., covenants) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc96dts5pvd9s8svwjq59yth))
    - Note: essentially you give them a loan in exchange for equity in the company, basic foundation of venture cap and private equity
- By contrast, bondholders rely only on public information and have little to no influence over an issuer’s operations. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc96hgaxdvkame3b8fccbdgj))
- The board of directors is responsible for protecting the interests of shareholders; hiring, firing, and setting the compensation of the firm’s senior managers; establishing the strategic direction of the firm; and monitoring the company’s financial performance and other aspects of its ongoing activities. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc96hcq4cf34amb7t27h5wb8))
- Board members include inside directors (e.g., senior executives, founders) and independent directors who have no material relationship with the company. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kc96kgnq4dq7yz62py8gxw86))
- While inside directors may have conflicts of interest with shareholders, independent directors may better protect shareholders’ interests. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7te6vg5rs6vbst3336phe))
- In a one-tier board structure, both inside and independent directors serve on a single board ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7tq0gehfh2x9g319kvnap))
- Major stock exchanges specify requirements for director independence, such as that most of the board should comprise independent directors ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7tvg7wf25ay1wd8zz994v))
- In continental Europe, boards have a two-tier structure in which the independent directors serve on a supervisory board that oversees a management board comprising inside directors ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7v3bwnb5p2223ag0nw7jm))
- Typically, board members are elected for a specified term (e.g., annual). In a staggered board, only a fraction of the board is elected each year. This decreases the power of shareholders to enact a major overhaul of the board ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7w6d35jr2pwee8fr66nsb))
- Firms with staggered boards justify them as providing continuity and allowing for a longer-term view of company strategy. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7wcr1v21xtmjk9s46wc4n))
- Senior managers typically receive compensation (remuneration) that is made up of a salary, a bonus based on some measure of company performance, and perks (e.g., expense accounts, use of company planes, special retirement benefits). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7xe4s1c33ar8erenv91b4))
- Their interests can be expected to include continued employment and maximizing the total value of their compensation. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7xsw0v4qwcftmf79gjyzw))
- Executive bonuses are typically tied to some measure of firm performance, giving senior managers a strong interest in the financial success of the firm. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7xtz7pc9rwmv3vb1892b9))
- Other employees are the human capital of the company, who provide their labor and skills. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7y0navqan5489c6aq72hk))
- Employees also have an interest in the sustainability and success of the firm. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7y3mxjy4e3fvmtb4q40mv))
- They have interests in their rate of pay, opportunities for career advancement, training, and working conditions. Employees may hold equity in the company by participating in employee stock participation plans, which are designed to align their interests with those of the shareholders of the company ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7ycx0tvnjecyw3s6gp2bk))
- In some industries, employees join unions to negotiate the terms of their employment. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7yhpnnpqkzkn3n26vznqq))
- Suppliers of resources to the firm have an interest in preserving an ongoing relationship with the firm, in the profitability of their trade with the firm, and in the growth and ongoing stability of the firm ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce7yymskfhwk985mbsprkkc))
- Because suppliers are typically short-term creditors of the firm, they also have an interest in the firm’s solvency and ongoing financial strength. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce81a39g4q24m53wt5d30fx))
- Customers rely on the company to provide a good or service of high quality at a reasonable price. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce81h5agkn79agskgnyvaw6))
- Customers may also have an interest in preserving an ongoing relationship to receive support and after-sale service. Maintaining the good will of customers is critical for the long-term prospects of any company. Customers increasingly care about environmental and social responsibilities of corporations. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce81qsh0cry5rghzsdgbxjn))
- Governments rely on corporations for tax revenue, economic growth, social welfare, and employment creation ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce81ws59bxx6nddc1qsqjp7))
- Regulators have an interest in ensuring compliance with various laws. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kce826ctg096d8p6nemhz6yz))
- Some debt and equity investors who take a stakeholder perspective are interested in evaluating companies’ environmental, social, and governance (ESG) factors. This is primarily due to three reasons: 1. Government stakeholders increasingly prioritize climate change and social policies through regulatory changes.
  2. ESG factors can have a material impact on companies’ results through potential loss of customer good will and financial losses due to fines and judgments. Poor corporate governance may lead to senior managers exploiting shareholders to advance personal interests.
  3. Many younger investors increasingly manage their wealth with ESG considerations in mind. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kcedxegpa8qhq3y22eqxr62p))
- Negative externalities arise when a company or its investors do not bear the full cost of its actions (e.g., environmental damage). Increased government regulations and stakeholder awareness necessitate companies to recognize these costs, either explicitly in their financial statements or implicitly. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kcedxpec7ywbeh5jc5n3qt8k))
- Environmental Factors ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kceep7sfp6nt7v4a5q0cmht2))
- Environmental factors include company contributions to problems such as climate change, air and water pollution, deforestation, energy efficiency, waste management, or water scarcity. Material environmental factors can have a substantial impact on companies’ operations or business models. Industries that are natural resource intensive have a direct impact on the environment, but other industries may have indirect impacts. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kceepk7qfnmm9m54hw91wewc))
- With respect to climate change, companies may face physical risk of adverse effects on assets or operations if severe weather increases in frequency, as well as transition risk as government regulations or consumer choices require switching from high-carbon to low-carbon activities. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kceg8cdhnkypkz32whq7xg8z))

- When one party hires another to carry out a task, the two parties are said to have a principal-agent relationship ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kcekd1v664yx2z09tmqmv7d2))
- In such a relationship a principal-agent conflict has the potential to arise, because an agent is hired to act in the interests of the principal, but an agent’s interests might not coincide exactly with those of the principal. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kcep45zw8nswaytjfh1jwhhc))
- For example, consider an insurance agent who is paid a commission on policies written. It would be in the agent’s interest to write insurance policies on people or property that are not good risks to maximize commission income. The principal (the owner of the insurance company) does not want to issue policies that are bad risks, as that is a money-losing proposition. Insurance companies mitigate this conflict by imposing underwriting standards for the policies they will issue and by continuing to work only with agents who consistently act in the company’s best interest. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kcexp7vtw6s37s81srvr064v))
- Agency costs are the costs of a principal-agent conflict. Agency costs can be direct, such as hiring employees to monitor the agent, or indirect, such as the opportunity cost of lost business. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kcsa4nbg91yj6j49en3m37cr))

- Some conflicts result from information asymmetry between shareholders and managers, which refers to the fact that managers have more and better information about the functioning of the firm and its strategic direction than shareholders do. - The CCC is calculated by adding the number of days it takes for a company to sell its inventory (days of inventory on hand), the number of days it takes for the company to collect payment from its customers (days sales outstanding), and the number of days it takes for the company to pay its suppliers (days payable outstanding). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn982fgswqjbkpyjq4mzn0p0))

- A lower CCC is generally better as it indicates that a company can generate cash quickly and efficiently, meaning that less of the company’s capital is devoted to working capital. A high CCC may indicate that a company is taking longer to convert its investments into cash, which could lead to cash flow problems and potentially limit the company’s ability to invest in growth opportunities. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn983ah97167g6kt3h3ygre2))
- A company can decrease its CCC by decreasing its inventories and receivables or by increasing (extending) its payables ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn983j6x1fb9hx503rcbyft0))
- Reduced inventories of raw materials may create production bottlenecks due to supply chain disruptions. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9840ee1ngjz5xebd8t0r88))

      when running a business, its important to keep an eye on the geopolitical situation for reasons like this. Its important that you know when to sotkc up your inventories expecting a adverse situation
- Reduced inventories of finished foods may mean an inability to meet spikes in customer demand. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9842t2g6dfx8c1t9g4mwdn))
    - Note: Again its all about making the best of the situation. if you dont have enough goods on hand, for reasons out of your control if there is a spike in demand you wont be able to meet it and grow.

- Forgoing the discount for prompt payment amounts to borrowing money from the supplier for (c – b) days, at an effective annual rate we can calculate as follows ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn991dr069xqnrek9myvkpqc))
- ![](https://readwise-assets.s3.amazonaws.com/media/reader/pub/cc701c4141a0a098cc6b691273b68d45.png) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn991x8mxedbwr84zrtxbebb))

    - Note: the company will save money by not borrowing from the vendors and suppliers but rather borrowing from the lender. 
      as the effective annual rate will make the borrowing a lot more expensive. 
      so its in the companies interests to pay before the discount ends to save money receive a discount and just take a loan from the borrower.
- EXAMPLE: EAR of supplier financing A supplier offers 2/10 net 30 terms. The bank interest rate is 8%. Which source of financing should the company prefer?
  Answer: Financing is for 30 − 10 = 20 days.
  EAR of supplier financing = (1 + 0.02 / 0.98)(365/20) − 1 = 0.446, or 44.6%.
  The cost of implicit supplier financing is much higher than the cost of explicit bank financing. The company should borrow from its bank at 8% to pay the invoice within 10 days. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9998w346r427x127e3mpte))

- MODULE 24.1: CAPITAL INVESTMENTS AND PROJECT MEASURES ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9btwvfds4pg5k3t77qev0c))
- The four types of capital investments include going concern projects, regulatory/compliance projects, expansion projects, and other projects ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9ca5hdd811bbvttdx3jyz4))
- Going concern projects may be needed to maintain the business or reduce costs. Projects that maintain the business do not require detailed analysis ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cac49vzvns5dsw14r9p3h))
    - Note: basically projects that the company needs to undertake to keep it going on the way it has been going
- The only issues are whether the existing operations should continue and, if so, whether existing procedures or processes should be maintained ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9caxtaxnmy1w1bh30t1nrt))
- Projects to improve efficiency may involve determining if equipment that is obsolete, but still usable, should be replaced. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cbmh796j2d3vrv3ae0yjc))
- To reduce financing risk, companies often use a match funding approach, financing projects with capital sources that are consistent with the project life. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cbtjg40sjwez0fry3e49r))
- Analysts often use a company’s annual depreciation expense as an estimate of the capital investment it needs for going concern projects ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9ccchm0r10805ryagmg1ex))
- Regulatory/compliance projects may be required by a government agency or insurance company and often involve safety-related or environmental concerns. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9ccqc24rh5gwvft2z85e24))
- These projects typically generate little to no revenue and require the company to evaluate alternative ways of carrying out the projects. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cdqs0pn1q89dv0762xgpt))
- Expansion projects grow the business and require a complex decision-making process that includes forecasting future demand. E ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9ce5v8fjk5xadkx2nz280m))
- Expansion projects can involve entering new markets or introducing new products within the same market. A detailed analysis including forecasting revenues and expenses is required. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9ceer7wsd310zwvwjjy1gg))
- Other projects, such as new investments outside a company’s existing lines of business, also entail a complex decision-making process with detailed analysis due to the uncertainty involved. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cem4wrtq1ve3z20xsc0qe))
- Other projects are often similar to startups that explore a new technology or business idea. These projects can also involve buying out an existing company in a new industry, which involve risks that include overpaying ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9certstn84st15k2zmfjjw))
- The capital allocation process is identifying and evaluating capital projects ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cfkcg5ckgw8fsayvhzz10))
- capital projects (i.e., projects where the cash flows to the firm will be received over a period longer than a year) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cfw3w7hgf4rjyntffa976))
- For a number of good reasons, capital allocation may be the most important responsibility that a financial manager has. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9chkwz9x1mdj827mkpbhvm))
- First, because a capital allocation decision often involves the purchase of costly long-term assets with lives of many years, the decisions made may determine the future success of the firm. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9chrn26ktfpbsdzacxaxqj))
- Second, the principles underlying the capital allocation process also apply to other corporate decisions, such as working capital management and making strategic mergers and acquisitions ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9chyg6pebnwxhqhcv5bfhg))
- Finally, making good capital allocation decisions is consistent with management’s primary goal of maximizing shareholder value. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cj1d65m7rn8zn1sfdpak6))
- Step 1: Idea generation. The most important step in the capital allocation process is generating good project ideas. Ideas can come from a number of sources, including senior management, functional divisions, employees, or sources outside the company. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cjdjnm6znskytc2sh8bar))
- Step 2: Analyzing project proposals. Because the decision to accept or reject a capital project is based on the project’s expected future cash flows, a cash flow forecast must be made for each project to determine its expected profitability. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cjjnfbvdpnmf00qsxkyv4))
- Step 3: Create the firm-wide capital budget. Firms must prioritize profitable projects according to the timing of the project’s cash flows, available company resources, and the company’s overall strategic plan. Many projects that are attractive individually may not make sense strategically. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cjvbkg1evcxtxtpwy3afr))
- Step 4: Monitoring decisions and conducting a post-audit. It is important to follow up on all capital allocation decisions. An analyst should compare the actual results to the projected results, and project managers should explain why projections did or did not match actual performance.
  Because the capital allocation process is only as good as the estimates of the inputs into the model used to forecast cash flows, a post-audit should be used to identify systematic errors in the forecasting process and improve company operations. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9ck2g2r0603c6s4k2fm6ak))
- Net present value (NPV) is the sum of the present values of all the expected incremental cash flows if a project is undertaken. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9ckdgt60e3tymf0entt8vd))
- The discount rate used is the firm’s cost of capital, adjusted for the risk level of the project ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cmxdw6h3fj7wq7nerpmt8))
- the NPV is the present value of the expected inflows minus the initial cost of the project. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cnxp00nqwc7q80b9bkpkm))
    - Note: esentially for th einvestment you are putting in does the future expected cash flow balance things out ?
- ![](https://readwise-assets.s3.amazonaws.com/media/reader/pub/5dee4396acfd0c0cbfb78519ed1502d8.png) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cq6vkra5mbc6t632f7sq6))
- A positive NPV project is expected to increase shareholder wealth, a negative NPV project is expected to decrease shareholder wealth, and a zero NPV project has no expected effect on shareholder wealth. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9cqcq3yqhdsvagpfrd2hs2))
    - Note: positive npv is a innovation project, negative npv can be a governmental required or adverse conditions company required project (project that company has to undertake to be able to continue operations) and a zero npv is a going concern project
- For independent projects, the NPV decision rule is simply to accept any project with a positive NPV and to reject any project with a negative NPV. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9d9s53dnk0bsg16vv185b4))
- For a normal project, the internal rate of return (IRR) is the discount rate that makes the present value of the expected incremental after-tax cash inflows just equal to the initial cost of the project. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9d025psppyfyyhth43tdb6))
- the IRR is the discount rate that makes the present value of a project’s estimated cash inflows equal to the present value of the project’s estimated cash outflows. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9d0a5qf4vz1csvb28mymj2))
- ![](https://readwise-assets.s3.amazonaws.com/media/reader/pub/a46a432c38a64cd8502875e99b9b2ecb.png) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9d0pmgd1p7btmcj870026x))
- ![](https://readwise-assets.s3.amazonaws.com/media/reader/pub/37aad36a8efeb45cf51ca6605f3e8843.png) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9d12thg16x2h552w0r91hc))
- IRR decision rule: If IRR is greater than the required rate of return, accept the project. If IRR is less than the required rate of return, reject the project. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9d9hpr9yqaa1dckf9k6tqk))
- The required rate of return for a given project is usually the firm’s cost of capital. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kn9dar2pxrtqfz61h85bxkac))
    - Note: as in does a project minimum cover the cost of the money borrowed to make the project ?
- a project analyst may adjust the required rate of return above or below the firm’s cost of capital to account for differences between the project’s risk and the average risk of all of the firm’s projects ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc0r0f1gwdy2422efg5hfr))
- For this reason, the minimum IRR above which a project will be accepted is often referred to as the hurdle rate. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc16kj6aj1hcrpzgcd23yc))
- Projects with IRRs above this rate will be accepted, while those with IRRs below this rate will not be accepted. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc1cabjp96bejrq1mcpvsn))
- A project has a conventional cash flow pattern if the sign on the cash flows changes only once, with one or more cash outflows followed by one or more cash inflows. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc2kt3jy1ah2anm209mxrg))
- An unconventional cash flow pattern has more than one sign change ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc2qd2as442vtbs50wsz27))
- Another example of unconventional cash flow is when the time intervals between cash flows differ ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc3aqb469gdbvqgseynhkc))
- A key advantage of NPV is that it is a direct measure of the expected increase in the value of the firm. In theory, a positive NPV project should cause a proportionate increase in a company’s stock price. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc65wxsdgwntdxgkjpkak3))
- A key advantage of IRR is that it measures profitability as a percentage, showing the return on each dollar invested ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc68pzf6n1vj2c33sankz6))
- The IRR provides information on the margin of safety that the NPV does not. From the IRR, we can tell how much below the IRR (estimated return) the actual project return could fall, in percentage terms, before the project becomes uneconomic (has a negative NPV). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc6cv6dnkf9ker0kad3dvv))
- The disadvantages of the IRR method are: ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc6w08sqj859w1y0fnkafn))
- It assumes that project’s cash flows are reinvested at the IRR while NPV assumes that those cash flows are reinvested at the project’s required rate of return. It is more realistic to assume the latter. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc70gbs9568fzbbgeg1m7x))
- For multiple sign changes, a project may have multiple IRRs that are difficult to interpret. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc79wdbq6qka3q6wafd30s))
- Return on Invested Capital ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc7w2st6zz9xff6cx1geeh))
- A company’s return on invested capital (ROIC), or simply return on capital, is defined ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc8h6acrkx0a90f5zrc23g))
- as its net operating profit after tax (NOPAT), or simply after-tax operating profit, divided by the average book value of its total capital over the period: ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc8rf253h5yfrz5bmb9n5e))
- ![](https://readwise-assets.s3.amazonaws.com/media/reader/pub/6667100f8a54fbb52b74de759c59456b.png) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbc96svb1r51h53gts6vnt9))
- After-tax operating profit is net income plus after-tax interest expense. We use this because we want to measure the return to all sources of capital (both debt and equity). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbca1kcr2c2etmwf9egx9hv))
- Invested capital includes long-term debt and equity, but excludes working capital. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcajm01c0mhfvkp6q1x18v))
- ![](https://readwise-assets.s3.amazonaws.com/media/reader/pub/86470a18d6e89a72f49d54a24f895bb6.png) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcaxcrqfkgw23wajqfh7p4))
- We refer to the ratio of after-tax operating profit to sales as operating margin after tax, ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcdrj6yjd4w4n8mzzw14kf))
- we refer to the ratio of sales to invested capital as capital turnover or asset turnover. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcdxkhk96arsjm1885nth8))
- This means that ROIC is the product of operating margin after-tax and invested capital turnover ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbce3ex02pgeq7pshw67kxc))
- A company can increase its ROIC by improving its operating margin or by increasing its capital turnover ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbce84arxjbgfrche1snt5s))
- Analysts compare ROIC to investors’ required rate of return. In this context, the required rate of return is a blended rate for both debt and equity. If ROIC is greater than the required rate, the firm is adding value over time. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcep03qmdka539s3df1ahp))
- There are three concerns with using ROIC: ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcfjnba2wkrkweay1hxz1j))
- 1. Because accounting treatments differ, ROIC may not be comparable across companies. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcfnhhjq2kq3rcyrdra5qd))
- 2. ROIC is backward-looking and can be volatile from year to year ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcfrdpgdd91tps9y3b0s4k))
- 3. Because ROIC is for the whole company, it may let profitable projects and good decisions mask unprofitable projects and poor decisions. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcfw0d5k5sm65tk4p7f21d))
- C ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcggfk6k84vz5srqcqy2q3))
- A. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbchpjv3jvcnmj34az3636r))
- MODULE 24.2: CAPITAL ALLOCATION PRINCIPLES AND REAL OPTIONS ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbchzef20fbch5p1vnawkn1))
- Principles of Capital Allocation ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcj1rhrmm1j2k10cjbem4r))
- Decisions are based on after-tax cash flows, not accounting income ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcmrdepfmt2j13xvvqj9s7))
- Accounting income is based on accruals and does not consider the timing of cash flows. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcmvdq57dfw1n41zs8q9hh))
- The impact of taxes must be considered when analyzing all capital allocation projects. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcnd8qg5j5j3d23qb25467))
- Any tax savings from non-cash tax deductions, such as depreciation and amortization, should also be included in the analysis. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcnqafmn4g0gx54pmekm2s))
- Incremental cash flows only. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcnvgcqr0m110qsfaeb3h3))
- Incremental cash flows are those that change if the project is undertaken. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbctpa01s432n2xm9c41yka))
- Sunk costs are costs that cannot be avoided even if the project is not undertaken. Because these costs are not affected by the accept/reject decision, they should not be included in the analysis. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbctym3w7yf47f9yw0bg6c9))
    - Note: sunk costs are costs that you will have to pay anyway to get th e basic project going
- An example of a sunk cost is a consulting fee paid to a marketing research firm to estimate demand for a new product before making a decision on the project. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcv5gzjp2yq8d7fx9m4571))
- Project analysis should consider the cash flow impact on other parts of the business. A negative effect, called cannibalization, occurs when a new project replaces sales from an existing product. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcw6mv8a2kmwx8vr90gy5c))
- An example of cannibalization is when a soft drink company introduces a diet version of an existing beverage. A ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcwk273d0nssydgfd6gdt6))
- A positive externality exists when doing the project would have a positive effect on sales of a firm’s other product lines. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcwn1nktqrv73p51rd49xm))
- The timing of cash flows is important. Capital allocation decisions account for the time value of money, which means that cash flows received earlier are worth more than cash flows to be received later. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcx2859ch99ka1p9xq1f1g))
- Cognitive Errors ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcxbd9dp2k7hfa2b8j6bf6))
- Poor forecasting. Examples include incorrectly allocating overhead costs or neglecting to anticipate how competitors will respond to a project.
  Video covering this content is available online. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcxrms98hg92m66gh2p8v0))
- Not considering the cost of internal funds. Often, the cost of internally generated funds is not accounted for (it should be same as cost of equity because those funds would have been paid as dividends if not for the project). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcyaqk8m32t8sh913dwjb4))
    - Note: the opportunity costs are not considered that this may not be an ideal way to spend this money at all
- Companies that have an aversion to paying dividends are potentially using the retained earnings to fund poor projects. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbcyf6q65nnwk4de62vp8g3))
    - Note: potentially being the key word here as alphabet notoriously almost never paid dividends 
      you culd argue given the number of projects they might start they are funding poor projects but yeah
- Incorrectly accounting for inflation. Firms can analyze projects either in nominal or real terms. An analysis based on real cash flows must also use a real discount rate. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbd1t1zzdx7hzzfzw7jderv))
- Behavioral Biases ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbd26pnjeprees91rtp44s6))
- Pet projects of senior management. Projects that have the personal backing of influential members of senior management may contain overly optimistic projections that make the project appear more profitable than it really is ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbd3m3a1qm7yrjcy16vdda9))
- the project might not be subjected to the same level of scrutiny as other projects. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbd4787hv5p7m71dhgdhvhz))
- Inertia in setting the entire capital budget ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbd4bjsjmv0cvw8y7xry8d0))
- Capital budgets for many companies are similar from one year to the next. This indicates anchoring of capital budgets to the prior year rather than appropriately considering what opportunities exist every year. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbd4xwtvp363dk10143k7fa))
- In a company with a culture of maximizing shareholder value, managers will return excess funds to shareholders whenever there is a lack of positive NPV projects and make a case for expanding the budget when there are multiple positive NPV opportunities ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbd56zwvt149we3rb1eq2gx))
- . Analysts should watch for companies with static or rising capital budgets coupled with declining returns as a sign of this bias. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbd5m0kwm3kbz9epq7v8hje))
- Basing investment decisions on EPS or ROE. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbd5sf2x8hq138wqch32537))
- Managers whose incentive compensation is tied to increasing EPS or ROE may avoid positive long-term NPV investments that are expected to reduce EPS or ROE in the short run. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbd5ya5pnet8agpr9gkj7jc))
    - Note: so more capita intensive projects or more time consuming projects that will pay of in the long run but might not pay off now
- Failure to generate alternative investment ideas. Generating investment ideas is a crucial step in the capital allocation process. However, once a manager comes up with a “good” idea, he or she may go with it rather than searching for an idea that is “better.” ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbd8be251vzqp2fgsb9anhe))
- Real options are future actions that a firm can take, given that they invest in a project today. Real options are similar to financial options (put and call options) in that they give the option holder the right, but not the obligation, to take a future action. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbdaq2sdrsne5yfx645ngwk))
- The value of real options could enhance a project’s NPV ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbdcc2jnvx1mjkvw9zfpedw))
- Options never have negative values because if, in the future, the specified action will have a negative value, the option holder will not take the action (i.e., not exercise the option). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbdcfa5zg925zwvdfhfkxey))
- Timing options allow a company to delay making an investment because it expects to have better information in the future. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbdcr0sqs3qx0r0gtcpcxee))
- Abandonment options allow management to abandon a project if the present value of the incremental cash flows from exiting a project exceeds the present value of the incremental cash flows from continuing the project ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbdcztgw0jczc66acestvmr))
- Expansion options or growth options allow a company to make additional investments in future projects if the company decides they will create value. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbdd5hmzpt11bc5cyn58r5q))
- Flexibility options give managers choices regarding the operational aspects of a project. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbddb0kzj9m3gy5n8x0fjj4))
- The two main forms are ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbddfzfnb45zxvrdgmkypeb))
- – Price-setting options allow the company to change the price of a product. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbddqe2w1mftd2qxjf77yhv))
- For example, the company may raise prices if demand for a product is high in order to benefit from that demand without increasing production. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbddxyf08me0x08ww7ftamd))
- Production-flexibility options may include paying workers overtime, using different materials as inputs, or producing a different variety of product. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbde3fd7w2483vfb3kxtj0v))
- Fundamental options are projects that are options themselves because the payoffs depend on the price of an underlying asset ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbdejkvzjpvqkpf8spkntfd))
- For example, the payoff for a copper mine is dependent on the market price for copper. If copper prices are low, it may not make sense to open a copper mine, but if copper prices are high, opening the copper mine could be very profitable. The operator has the option to close the mine when prices are low and open it when prices are high. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbdf8cbcek2x093t0je7g14))

- C. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbdj89bdx66f21r0e0jwa0s))

- CAPITAL STRUCTURE ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbe1m5cbk0ssss1gd7vwbdx))
- MODULE 25.1: WEIGHTED-AVERAGE COST OF CAPITAL ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbe1sy29qhbjktnbtk3mnfm))
- The cost of debt is lower than the cost of equity because debt has priority of claims over equity ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbe31sg83ceqnd5zvt8rfe0))
- Typically a company will target a capital structure that minimizes its WACC, while also considering the nature of ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbmdjn3awhghjt6kkrjhmn8))
- its assets (i.e., long-lived or shorter-term) when choosing the duration of its financing sources. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbmdn5q9t7fsm83445b5eaa))
- We may view factors that affect capital structures as those that determine a company’s capacity to service debt. These factors may be internal or external to a firm ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbmhdt6t7w2g1m1ajtakgd4))
- Internal factors include the characteristics of the business or industry, a company’s life cycle stage, a company’s existing debt level, and the corporate tax rate. External factors include market and business cycle conditions, regulation, and industry norms. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbmhtb4qncmm3mtfbv47d05))
- Company characteristics that influence the proportion of debt in a company’s capital structure ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbmn4vk6vnga8jk4pvmd92n))
- Growth and stability of revenue ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbmn8fk7bq8x5mapxqrf3p3))
- High growth of revenue or stability of significant revenue suggest a continuing ability to service debt. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbmne9hwxmwc0yderc1qed8))
- Growth and predictability of cash flow. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbn60pmm3wvyfx8r2h7f6wn))
- Growing cash flow increases the ability to service debt ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbn6715b01ev7z24sy4nr1x))
- Significant and stable cash flows indicate a continuing ability to service debt. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbn93dbnc6m60dj3y36p2h9))
- Amount of business risk. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbn96g8zt5kext772mhrahn))
- More business risk (operational risk and demand risk) means greater variability of earnings and cash flows, which decreases the ability to service debt. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbn9azy76nv7kapdj24xwy4))
    - Note: making equity a lot more attractive 
      High risk high reward
- Amount and liquidity of company assets ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01knbnbedq1kx904r7m4m9kpjx))
- Assets can be pledged as collateral to make a company’s debt more attractive. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3v7d9k2g1bdj93xe2q0ddp))
- Cost and availability of debt financing. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3v7h2xsazab9nkd65p17x3))
- Companies find debt relatively more attractive when the cost of debt is lower and investors are more willing to lend to the company. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3v7rc0tk61b079g5vattn5))
- In general, the more stable, predictable, and recurring are a company’s revenues and cash flows, the higher proportion of debt it can have in its capital structure ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3v80pv3kws9r2h05gqffn1))
- Companies in noncyclical industries are better able to support high proportions of debt than companies in cyclical industries. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3v87d9c2df105gs222m1rc))
- Companies with low fixed operating costs as a proportion of total costs (i.e., low operating leverage) are better able to support high proportions of debt than companies with high fixed costs. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3va52d4emtda7bwzk6qrst))
- Companies with subscription-based revenue models are better able to support high proportions of debt than companies with pay-per-use revenue models. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3va8c87pwgt7hy8ytx4y1r))
- creditors tend to view tangible assets as better collateral than intangible assets, especially when those assets can be sold for cash, if necessary, without losing significant value (i.e., are more liquid) or are more readily substituted for similar assets (i.e., are more fungible). ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3whc0f48eaf6mebvhhkvp4))
    - Note: in most cases funglible assets are also incredibly liquid like gold or bonds.
      certain other not very liquid assets can be viewed as fungible like oil diamons etc, but they are under contention
- A company that owns its productive assets outright as opposed to using assets owned by others (such as a franchise model) has more collateral, which improves access to debt financing and reduces borrowing costs. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wk8hfgptdjxx6k6m2qwer))
- Leverage (e.g., debt-to-equity or debt-to-operating profit) and coverage ratios (e.g., interest coverage = EBIT / interest expense) are used for analyzing debt capacity. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wnaz0ap6967bkwardf2wc))
- 1. Start-up stage. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wq6xjkp8thrph2r5beva2))
- Sales are just beginning, and operating earnings and cash flows tend to be low or negative. Business risk is relatively high. Company debt is quite risky and, if issued, would require high interest rates. Assets, both accounts receivable and fixed assets, typically are low; therefore, they are not available as collateral for debt. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wq8k953gpnf56b9zpcxm1))
- For these reasons, start-up companies are financed almost exclusively with equity. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wprb7v99h5hajr1d94mtm))
- In some cases, high-growth companies with rapidly rising stock prices may find it possible to issue convertible debt, which allows the company to use a lower-cost source of capital and avoid immediate dilution of existing shareholders’ interests. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wpxnyc8xk3cwacxmr0fc0))
- Leasing assets is another source of debt financing available to companies in this stage of the life cycle. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wpzee8zmwb0f6krt1q0xg))
- 2. Growth stage. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wq29pm6rjy9rzc2d7fn95))
- Revenue and cash flow are rising, and business risk is somewhat reduced. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wqewha4anhzhcdfgz6cbm))
- Debt financing cost is somewhat reduced (i.e., usage is conservative), and investors may be willing to lend to the company, often with the loans secured by fixed assets or accounts receivable ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wqnhdsas29c60vxna073n))
- 3. Mature stage. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wqrsgtq0d077qappcfxmh))
- In this stage, revenue growth is slowing and business risk is much lower. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wqxprkjcasjvs2masmvxf))
- Cash flow is significant and relatively stable ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wqzm5p09pw352zd7a7std))
- Debt financing, including unsecured debt, is widely available at a relatively low cost. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wr1vd2z2q15gx1v8v4gyy))
- The cost of capital is also influenced by top-down factors ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wr9tnn06fwy8zddfjma4g))
- Top-down factors are macroeconomic factors that affect the benchmark interest rate (e.g., U.S. Treasury rate) and credit spreads to benchmark rates. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wrfbccgh7g5ykrsk3tf5v))
- These include inflation, the real GDP growth rate, monetary policy, and exchange rates ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wrjbpzkwxnehzvghjqr8h))
- In business cycle downturns, debt investors demand greater yield spreads to benchmark bonds from corporate borrowers to compensate them for the increased risk of default ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wrsgpbjgg8dkts10m2rnm))
- This is especially true for companies in cyclical industries. Some industries may be favorably affected by the economic environment ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wrz7gp2t801mh03pe4j1m))
- For example, oil industry profitability is linked to oil prices, and spreads for issuers in the industry tend to narrow when oil prices rise. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3ws31t85m5bn1qms9v17fr))

- MM Proposition I (No Taxes): Capital Structure Irrelevance ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3wst3e538ykvkz8j4d413d))
    - Note: simply states that given all assumptions of perfectly competitive markets and perfectly operating world. 
      the cost of debt and equity is equal. 
      So makes no difference as 40% equity, 60% debt = 60% equity, 40% debt
      since all the ebit is available to the debt and equtity holders, if a person woned all of the debt and equity he would be entitled to all of the cashflow
- This result is referred to as MM I. The assumptions that lead to MM I are as follows ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3x0anj8v2d2ty0k4rt4gvz))
- Capital markets are perfectly competitive. There are no transactions costs, taxes, or bankruptcy costs ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3x0fcrt60ddy7r2yzgb9xv))
- Investors have homogeneous expectations. They have the same expectations with respect to cash flows generated by the firm. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3x0ma0g8ph8h3cx7f0v890))
    - Note: means its in all the investors benefit to think the same way about the future
- There is riskless borrowing and lending. Investors can borrow and lend at the risk- free rate. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3x4nffc5pqqcgn923v0pkw))
- There are no agency costs. There are no conflicts of interest between managers and shareholders. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3x4tjtyabjhaj86fn6jsm7))
- Investment decisions are unaffected by financing decisions. Operating income is independent of how the firm is financed. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3x520y851k4zdxx7ab6195))
- Intuitively, we can explain MM I in terms of a pie. MM I essentially concludes that the amount of pie available (value of the firm) does not depend on how it is sliced (the capital structure). The value of a firm does not change depending on how the claims to its earnings are divided. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3xbhhfs5xk563bb4rhtb6m))
- ![](https://readwise-assets.s3.amazonaws.com/media/reader/pub/a16a7051572d63385b333df0e4cf47d3.png) ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3xbs4jwxpekatmbcs97kwb))
- Consider why the pie analogy holds. The operating earnings (EBIT) of a firm are available to providers of capital. In a company with no debt, all of the operating earnings are available to equity holders, and the value of the company is the discounted present value of these earnings, with a discount rate that depends on the risk of ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3xcj28vr9n03wjjh1z91cr))
- (uncertainty about) earnings. If a company is financed partly by debt and partly by equity, operating earnings are divided between debtholders and equity holders. If one entity purchased all the debt and all the equity of the firm, it would be entitled to all of the EBIT of the firm, so the total value of its holdings must equal the value of an all- equity firm—which also has a claim to all the operating earnings of the firm. Under the assumption that operating earnings are unaffected by financing decisions, the total value of debt and equity will be unaffected by the proportions of debt and equity in a firm’s capital structure. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3xcmxn20mmvvyyrst52jmq))
- MM Proposition II: Cost of Equity and Leverage ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp3xcvt0sjhghh2z8phk1mh0))
- MM’s second proposition (MM II) is framed in terms of a firm’s cost of capital, rather than firm value. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp5svxz1eemv1tpcwc9sat31))
- t ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kpe2rtt950trwk4yqw8kdgaw))
- Based on the same assumptions as MM I, MM II states that the cost of equity increases linearly as a company increases its proportion of debt financing. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp5sw36fx96fprrq37x3kqpa))
    - Note: as debt holder gets the first claim on assets and cashflow. As debt increases so does the cost of equity financing as the assets and cashflow are bing collateralised in debt, the risk to the equity holders rises. 
      As the risk rises so does the cost of the equity
- The cash flows promised to bondholders have priority, so that equity holders receive the remaining cash flows after the claims of debtholders have been met. Because the cash flows promised to debtholders are more certain (less risky) than the residual cash flows promised to equity holders, the cost of debt will be less than the cost of equity. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp5thr7zm96dfastysffpjnd))
- The greater the amount of debt in a firm’s capital structure, the more uncertain are the residual cash flows to equity holders ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp5tr8bfz4ebcxrhqq4r5kfz))
- MM II tells us that as companies increase the proportion of debt financing, the risk of the cash flows to equity holders increases, which increases the cost of equity. ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp5trd7bzr3gaffbgxrv1grz))
- The conclusion of MM II is that the decrease in financing costs from using a larger proportion of (lower-cost) debt is just offset by the increase in the cost of equity, resulting in no change in the firm’s WACC ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp5v6jmv7hgkzvwfy2e16n55))
    - Note: essentially decrease in borrowing costs by using debt, which is generally cheaper than anything else depending on the stage of the company is offset by the increased costs of equity therefore there is no change in the cost of capital so you arent finding a loophole in the grand scheme of things 
      wonder how many things are like this where you think that you have found a loophole when you really havent

- o ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp5vctg7k6mahj4wwr0bqyf4))
- As leverage (the debt-to-equity ratio) increases, the cost of equity increases, but the cost of debt and WACC are unchanged ([View Highlight](https://read.readwise.io/read/01kp5vcqj24ybt4jraz9m91ghc))
